ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e188	The case concerns consent to the conclusion of an agreement with the European Union on the extension of trade in agricultural products under Article 19 of the EEA Agreement. The agreement is of particular importance, so that the consent of the Storting to the conclusion of the agreement is necessary in accordance with the second paragraph of Section 26 of the Constitution. Article 19 of the EEA Agreement requires Norway and the EU to review the trading conditions for agricultural products. Agreements have previously been concluded with effect from 1 July 2003. The government decided in 2008 to start negotiations with the EU on trade in agricultural products. A new agreement was reached on 28 January 2010 with Norway and the European Commission. The Agreement was subsequently approved in the EU by Commission Decision of 27 September 2010 and Council Decision of 8 November 2010. The Agreement shall be submitted to the European Parliament for approval. The Article 19 agreement will not be implemented until the middle of 2011, i.e.: in the current calendar year. It is assumed that new tariff quotas resulting from the agreement will be allocated on a pro rata basis from the date of entry into force until 31 December of this year. There is an overall committee which points to an ever-increasing increase in imports of agricultural products into Norway. While the import value in 2000 was 16.57 billion. kr, the corresponding amount in 2009 amounted to a total of 33,8 billion. kr. Of this, the entire 68 pst came. in 2009 from the EU territory. An overall committee also points out that the outcome of the Article 19 negotiations will lead to an increase in trade in agricultural products between the EU and Norway. Article 19 requires reductions in barriers to trade to be mutually beneficial. This is an important principle that needs to be shown greater respect by Norwegian authorities in the future. It is generally stated in the Norwegian political debate that organised imports from the least developed countries – so-called MUL countries – should be prioritised in the future, rather than tolerations from EU countries. It is also an overall committee which points to the fact that the negotiated agreement would involve an increase in the annual proceeds for the State on an uncertain basis of 120 million. kr. The background for this is that the expected increase in imports far exceeds the expected export potential, inter alia. as a result of Norway’s overall high level of earnings and costs. There is also an overall committee which will point out that the conclusion of the agreement involves the establishment of 13 new import quotas for Norway and the extension of 10 existing import quotas. The Agreement concerns in particular two important and sensitive product areas for Norwegian agriculture by increasing the import quotas for cheese and meat by 2 700 and 3 100 tonnes respectively. Most new allowances are allocated by auction, which is a sound principle. Provision should be made to increase the share of future auctions, as an overall committee is in favour. Auction fees will vary according to the circumstances. The market situation in Norway, the relationship between production and demand, is important. Increased cheese quotas come in a situation where today's regular imports are higher than the duty-free quota. For poultry, uncertainty related to the salmonella situation in EU countries has a bearing on the utilisation of the import quota. It is a joint committee which accedes to the negotiated agreement. There is a majority, all except for the members of the Progress Party and the Right, who indicate that the Government is, and will continue to be, a supporter of Norwegian agricultural policy. The protection of imports is a prerequisite for ensuring the disposal of Norwegian agricultural products and the achievement of fixed prices in the agricultural agreement. The majority show that the government considers that the various international negotiations and other national processes relating to import protection need to be viewed in the context. This concerns both WTO negotiations and negotiations on bilateral agreements within both Article 19 of the EEA Agreement and Protocol 3 and EFTA, as well as our import regime for trade with developing countries. The majority have noticed that government emphasis has been placed on seeing the negotiations in the context of developments in WTO negotiations, which have been ongoing in parallel. The effects of these agreements must be such that they are compatible with the agricultural policy objectives of the government. The last one is central. The protection of imports ensures a national policy and a national price level. It is completely parallel to what the government is doing to ensure the pay and working conditions of employees in Norway. It is therefore absolutely central that one should ensure a business that is significantly based on prices in the market.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e205	The EU is Norway’s main trading partner. About 80 pst. of total Norwegian exports go to the EU, and about 70 pst. of our imports come from the EU. The EEA Agreement provides Norway with access to the EU internal market and is therefore of major importance for Norwegian business. The Agreement provides for the free movement of goods, services, persons and capital throughout the EEA territory and ensures a level playing field for Norwegian activities as elsewhere in the EU. The EEA Agreement provides for trade in agricultural products within the EEA territory. This is reflected in Article 19 of the EEA Agreement on basic agricultural products and Protocol 3 on processed agricultural products. Article 19 of the EEA Agreement provides that Norway and the EU are to review, at intervals of two years, the conditions governing trade in basic agricultural products with a view to gradual liberalisation. Trade shall be mutually beneficial. The first Article 19 agreement was launched on 1 July 2003. Norway and the EU started in 2006 preparations for new negotiations on expanded trade in agricultural products under Article 19. The government has, on the basis of Soria Moria, awaited the WTO negotiations before the conclusion of the negotiations, but as they have drawn up in time, a new agreement has now been concluded. The agreement has been approved by the Commission and the Council and is submitted to the Stortinget and the EU Parliament for approval before implementation. The government assumes that import protection is, and will continue to be, a supporter of Norwegian agricultural policy. Import protection is a prerequisite for ensuring the disposal of Norwegian agricultural products and the achievement of fixed prices in the agricultural agreement as a basis for satisfactory income development in agriculture. The impact of different import facilitations must therefore be at a level consistent with the government's agricultural policy objectives. The results of the Article 19 negotiations will lead to a certain increase in trade in agricultural products between the EU and Norway. On the part of the government, emphasis has been placed on seeing the negotiations in the context of developments in the WTO negotiations which have been ongoing in parallel and where any WTO agreement would involve a significant market opening to Norway also for sensitive Norwegian agricultural products. In general, Norway has concentrated in the negotiations on concessions for agricultural products where there are already imports at regular customs duties or where the food industry already has an import need. Concessions have also been granted for goods that may contribute to enhancing the diversity of the Norwegian food market. Central elements of the agreement are cheese and meat. For cheese, there is a reciprocal increase in the existing cheese quota, and the whole cheese quota is duty-free. Today, a significant quantity of cheese is also imported into Norway with normal customs duties. This will probably continue to be done despite the increased duty-free quantity of cheese in the agreement. For meat, temporary EU import quotas have been established for bovine, poultry and pigs. Part of the quota increase for meat is justified by emphasising food specialities and consumer considerations. Some new or increased quotas have been established into Norway for the Norwegian food industry. This applies to iceberg lettuce, frozen strawberries, potatoes for snack production, sunflower juice and concentrate, blueberry concentrate and apple juice and concentrate. In addition, a new quota has been opened for early potatoes before the Norwegian season. These allowances cover existing imports without pre-empting Norwegian production. In addition, the agreement involves duty-free or duty-free allowances entering the EU market for, inter alia, vegetable oils, berries, snacks, dog and cat food, tears and Christmas trees. These quotas will realistically allow Norwegian farms to benefit. Up to the EU, a new quota of 400 tonnes raspberries has been opened. In addition, the import period has been extended for sweet cherries and morsels. These changes have occurred after industry involvement, which sees export opportunities here. In addition, Norway and the EU have granted mutual exemption from customs duties on products where there are currently very low customs duties. A total of 186 trade lines or parts of trades have been freed from customs duty. This represents barely 14 pst of all agricultural products in the Norwegian customs tariff. Norway also has limited export opportunities for agricultural products due to high cost levels. The agreement will involve the parties as soon as possible bringing together all previous exchanges of letters between Norway and the EU into a consolidated exchange of letters. The Parties agree to look at the possibilities for enhanced cooperation in the geographical indication of origin of agricultural products and to go through trade in agricultural products about two years after the entry into force of this Agreement. I recommend that Stortinget approve this agreement on extended trade in agricultural products under Article 19 of the EEA Agreement.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e244	The government assumes that the protection of imports is and will continue to be a pillar of Norwegian agricultural policy. Import protection is a prerequisite for ensuring the provision of Norwegian agricultural products, obtaining fixed prices in the agricultural agreement and exploiting the resources available to us for food production. This is also the basis for satisfactory income development in agriculture. The sum of trade agreements and the management of import protection are essential for the possibility of Norwegian food production. Therefore, different international negotiations and other national processes relating to import protection must be considered in the context. This concerns both WTO negotiations and negotiations on bilateral agreements within both the EEA and EFTA, as well as market openings to Norway through our import regime for trade with developing countries. The overall impact of different import facilitations must be at a level consistent with the objectives of agricultural policy. I am pleased that the majority of the Committee, all except the members of the Progress Party and the Right, subscribe to these government assessments. The starting point for Norway in the Article 19 negotiations has been that we continue to have strong import protection. We have therefore first and foremost granted to the European Union tolerances for products where this is beneficial to the Norwegian food industry and where there are already imports at normal customs duties. At the same time, the new agreement opens up slightly more imports of food specialities that will complement the sample of products in the Norwegian daily food market. For some companies, the agreement will involve reduced commodity costs through reduced customs duties, but will not be at the expense of existing Norwegian raw material production. In the course of the negotiations, we have had close contact with agricultural and industrial organisations in order to best assess the impact on the industry of the agreement. The agreement also increases export opportunities for Norwegian agricultural holdings. Norwegian exports of agricultural products are traditionally very modest, but for some goods the agreement provides market opportunities that are realistic for Norwegian enterprises to benefit from. The agreement, in my opinion, is well anchored in the government's agricultural policy, although it may also pose some challenges. The main challenges lie in the increased import quotas for cheese and meat. For cheese, the duty-free quotas between Norway and the EU have been mutually increased to 7 200 tonnes. This means that a greater share of existing imports of cheese from the EU – about 8 000 tonnes – will be duty-free. For meat, increased import quotas have been allocated to Norway of a total of 3100 tonnes. Of these, 2,300 tonnes of temporary quotas for beef, pigmeat and poultry meat will be included in any future WTO quota if the WTO agreement comes into force. This Agreement is, as I said, the other Agreement concluded in accordance with Article 19 of the EEA Agreement. The first agreement was concluded on 1 July 2003. For me, it has been important for the new agreement to be anchored within the framework of Norwegian agricultural policy. At the same time, we have concluded a comprehensive agreement. I expect that this will also be the conclusion when, in retrospect, about two years after the implementation of the agreement, we are going to go through the trade in agricultural products between Norway and the EU. I have noticed that the committee is positive that most new import quotas will be allocated by auction. From my point of view, it's a much better way of distributing than a division based on historical rights, as we have partly for cheese. I have also noticed that the Committee believes that it should be facilitated in the future to increase the share of auctions. Finally, I welcome the fact that a unanimous committee is joining the negotiated agreement.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e351	Today, we are to adopt the largest simplification measure for Norwegian small businesses ever. We are dealing here with the legal proposition 51 L and Document 8:19 S regarding the statutory auditor's obligation and the retention period for the accounting document. For a hundred years we have had statutory audits of all companies. As of this year, we will exempt the small companies. This is a simplification for business. That's good business policy, and that's good red-green policy. With this proposal, these companies will be able to assess for themselves whether the costs associated with auditing exceed the audited accounts of the own-account company. The audit obligation selection was identified at kgl. Resolution of 11 May 2007 to address, inter alia: exemptions from the audit obligation for small enterprises. The selection presented its evaluation in 2008. The majority argued that the audit obligation should be passed on to all limited liability companies, while a minority went in to exempt the smallest companies. The distribution went on a wide-ranging basis with a deadline of October 2008. The majority stressed the importance of the audit for proper tax accounting and for the prevention and detection of financial crime. For the minority in the selection, it was decisive that audit costs are significant for small enterprises, with a background in EU rules, simplification, the need to reduce administrative costs, especially for smaller enterprises, and a desire to remove cost-requiring rules that do not have a corresponding social benefit effect. When we have chosen to exempt small limited liability companies from auditing obligations, there has been a balance between the importance of tax control and the fight against financial crime on the one hand and the importance of simplification and international development on the other. There are good, factual reasons to carry on the audit obligation, but the simplification of Norwegian small businesses has had a decisive impact. In addition, it has been important to ensure equality between Norwegian limited liability companies and Norwegian registered foreign companies, NUF, in relation to the audit obligations. The proposal exempts limited liability companies which fulfil the following criteria: annual revenue less than 5 million. kr balance sheet total less than 20 million. kr average number of added that do not exceed 10 years In addition, the General Assembly must decide on the use of the audit exemption. 136 086 limited liability companies meet these three criteria. But among these, there are 16,954 parent companies that are not covered by the exemption. In addition, there are 25,688 pure subsidiaries among the 136,086 limited liability companies. The total number of limited liability companies that can opt out of the audit will then be 119 132. In isolation, the legislative proposal does not impose an audit obligation on a subsidiary below the thresholds. It cannot be excluded that the statutory auditor will have to have any work with the subsidiary, but there is no need for a full audit. And it's important for me to make it clear that the government is not ordering the audit of a subsidiary. It is well possible that some companies will keep auditing. In that case, it is voluntary. To Document 8:19 S for 2010–2011 on custody obligations: The Minister of Finance has told him to come back with a bill on this later this spring. But here the question will also be about the control options on one side and simplification/saving for the companies on the other side. This must be weighed against each other, and questions must be asked about whether the savings for businesses are so important that they are more important than control. This is what we're going to come back to. The red-green government sees this as part of its active industrial policy. On Friday she presented a new ownership message. We are hearing a proposal on EDAY, electronic dialogue with employers. The government will continue its good work – for businesses and for good industrial policy. Today, therefore, we are to adopt exemptions from the audit obligation for small companies. I met one who has worked in the accounting and auditing industry since 1976, and he said that this was the biggest simplification measure he had ever experienced. And it's the simplification measures of the times for Norwegian small businesses, which also involve potential savings in the billions class. Enterprises will be able to save between NOK 10 000 and NOK 30000 separately in audit expenditure per year. This is in addition to a number of measures taken by the government to reduce costs and burdens for Norwegian business. I recommend this option from the Financial Committee. I also mention a proposal that is not currently distributed, but which the Secretariat has received.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e400	Since I was elected to the Stortinget in 2005, re-elected in 2009, and during all these years I have been a member of the Financial Committee, of course I have been in contact with those in Norway who run small businesses, and not least their stakeholder organisations. One of their main battles has been to cancel the audit obligation for those with less than 5 million. kr on the market, which will benefit its founders and small companies. That is why the Progress Party has fully supported this and has made proposals on it in the past. I have even had the pleasure of participating in several political panel debates over the years where this has been the subject, and where the audience and often the organizer of the debate have been the supporters of voluntary audit. But I have also been in debate several times there more or less 99 pst. by the audience, and the organiser of the debate — often bureaucrats — has been headstrong and extremely negative to such a simplification solution. The interesting observation has been that my counter-debateants, like the Workers' Party and the Socialist Left, have almost reproached the proposal of the Progress Party that it should be voluntary for small businesses to have auditors. But this is often the case when the People's Party is promoting proposals. As far as I am concerned, I am very pleased that today we are going to amend the law of auditors and some other laws, including: exemptions from the statutory duty on small limited liability companies. There's going to be a majority of these proposals. The government's reverse operation, which now proposes that the smallest limited liability companies be able to decide for themselves whether it is appropriate for them to have an audit, is fully supported by business associations, such as: NHO, Business Association, Norway’s Authorised Accounting Association – NARF – and Norway’s Bondelag. However, the latter organisation, Norway's Bondelag, and we in the Progress Party believe that responsible companies with less than 5 million. kr on the market, should also be exempted from revision, even if the number of participants exceeds five. In this way, I am somewhat surprised that the Central Party does not support the proposal of the Advance Party, the Right Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left. This type of company is relatively common in agriculture and forestry, and the centre party in election campaigns tends to be a champion for it. As far as other forms of enterprise are concerned, I miss discussion and proposals on simplification and cost savings in terms of auditing. I'm thinking, for example. on foundations. Often these are foundations aimed at caring for and rehabilitating peaceful buildings. Volunteers may have spent several thousand hours working without charging. The equity of the foundations can be anything from NOK 1 to NOK 100000. In that case, I find it unreasonable that the State should impose these audit obligations. I also think that the same should be considered in terms of ownership alliances, housing law and housing law. Townyards with common courtyards and other common areas are often organised as a pool. Maybe there are shops, a couple of offices and some apartments. Then it is usually not more than NOK 100000 or NOK 200000 in turnover per year. Nevertheless, such a group is required to have an auditor. This means about a year. NOK 15 000 to the accountant and perhaps NOK 15 000 to NOK 20 000 to the accountant. There is a very large extra cost, which leads to even greater common costs for those who live in this townyard or who run shops there. So I have a hope that during this debate the Council of State will announce that he will present a case of voluntary auditing for corporate forms other than limited liability companies. Another disappointment is the counter-declaration that fewer limited liability companies do not audit than was the intention. I am thinking about the parent company in small groups, which should also be exempted from the statutory obligation. That means that the simplification is not as big as the government first gave the impression. Nearly 17 000 parent companies will not discharge their audit obligations even if they are smaller than the limits set. In addition, the auditor will probably have to look at the approx. 26,000 subsidiaries. In this debate, we also have a proposal from the Progress Party on other aspects of the audit obligation. We propose to reduce the retention requirement for accounting material from 10 to 5 years and to propose amendments to adjacent legislation where the provisions affect the retention obligation. In conclusion, I would like to address these proposals and the proposals we are promoting together with others.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e481	The removal of the audit obligation for small axis companies is a matter the Right has had on its program, on its wish list, for a very long time. The removal of the audit obligation is a significant contributor to making everyday life simpler for thousands of Norwegian small businesses, not least for part of those who have chosen to organise themselves either as a NUF abroad or as an individual enterprise, and is one of several obstacles that make people not allowed to start their own company because there are too many forms and because there are too many start-up costs and operating costs before actually getting revenue. That's why this is a good day for small businesses, and it's good that the government, for once, also does what the Right wants, and that the Right has wanted for a long time. The Auditor Committee, the NHO Committee, proposed this in 2008. It was therefore high time that the matter was brought before Stortinget. Simplification is important for the Right. A simpler Norway with fewer forms is an essential factor in the well-being and further development of entrepreneurs and small businesses, thus creating growth for the whole of Norway. We want to remove the audit obligation for small businesses with less than 5 million. kr in turnover and less than 10 employees, as we do today. Of course, there will be demands on the balance sheet and that the General Assembly and the Governing Board also have a responsibility. We will also continue to support the current struggle to reduce the requirement to share capital for the limited companies. There has been a proposal to reduce it to NOK 30 000. That seems to be a good idea. We have also participated in several proposals today to reduce the requirement for keeping accounting documents. And we see that in the NOU on which this case is based, there are 80 special certification requirements imposed on small businesses, which otherwise the government will also have a new review of now, to make it somewhat easier. For the right, it is also important to have an increased focus on electronic solutions via Altin and other things, as well as to have them. also has the necessary capacity for business. In the past, the right has also made a proposal to introduce a Swedish model regulatory council to make it somewhat more difficult for bureaucracy to come up with good proposals for new rules. We think maybe a rule board would have stopped the famous bathroom proposal for the red-green government, which assumes that you need 20 forms to replace a tile in your bathroom. Enterprises that have had a general meeting before the law is implemented, i.e. reasonably soon, namely, 1st quarter 2011, may need to have a new general meeting in order to avoid an obligation to audit, even if they decide to do so before the law enters into force. We have, in a note, asked the government to take this into account, so that double work is avoided and the intention of the bill is respected, namely that this should be a real simplification. Responsible companies with a turnover of less than 5 million kr is currently exempt from audit obligations, but the obligation occurs if there are more than five owners, or if there are, for example. are different ASs that own the ANS. It is proposed that the NOU should also exempt these from the audit obligation, because they fulfil the intentions of less than 5 million. kr in turnover, and because the balance sheet requirement is – what to say – met, and the corresponding in relation to employees. There is a common form of organisation in agriculture, particularly in forestry, and even the Ministry of Justice has proposed to abolish their audit obligations. It is therefore strange that the Centre Party does not contribute to following up the interests of agriculture on a case that has been raised by both the NOU and which today invites the Centre Party to participate in. What's a little worse for us is that when the Ministry and the Minister of Finance and the Minister of Industry launched this, this was going to apply to 137 000 companies. Then it comes to the conclusion that by definition, parent companies – even parent companies that have no tasks other than owning shares in another company – do not have any other activity – are subject to an audit obligation. So they get an increased cost. That means that one has 17,000 parent companies, and logically they have at least one subsidiary. ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e599	First, I would like to thank the case rapporteur for helping to bring this important matter forward. In the Central Party's programme of action for this process of enlargement, we have an important industrial policy simplification reform. It's called in our program: ‘The central lot will remove the audit obligation for small enterprises.’ A long-term, hard work on the matter has been completed when the government now implements this. The solution means that all limited liability companies that have both less than 5 million. SEK in revenue, less than 20 million. kr in balance sheet total, total equity and liabilities, and the average number of employees carrying out no more than 10 annual works is allowed to opt out of auditing the annual accounts. This means a significant relief in administrative costs for the small limited liability companies. About 140000 limited liability companies will be able to avail themselves of the audit exemption, which is about 68 pst. of all the limited liability companies in Norway. The average audit cost is estimated to be between NOK 10000 and NOK 15000 for these companies. Overall, it could mean between 1.5 and 2.5 billion. kr in savings for the small limited liability companies. While small limited liability companies are exempted from the audit requirement, Norwegian registered non-resident companies, so-called NUFs, will also be subject to audit requirements when the activity exceeds the referenced figures for Norwegian limited liability companies. The matter has been thoroughly discussed among the government parties, where the consideration of tax control and the fight against financial crime on the one hand, and the consideration of simplification and economic saving for companies on the other, have been dilemmas that need to be resolved. Once the audit obligation has been removed, simplification and savings for public limited liability companies are the most weighty. However, with the removal of the audit obligation, the companies are free to adopt at the general meeting if they still wish to engage the auditor, thereby meeting the claims of, for example, creditors. This reform implies an equal treatment of public limited liability companies and NUF companies in terms of auditing, which contributes to a weakening of the interest in using the NUF as a corporate form. For the Center Party, this is a desirable development. The centre party has long seen the NUF as an unfortunate form of company based on its head office position abroad, and thus associated difficulties for Norwegian tax authorities to control their activities. The reform will make it easier for entrepreneurs to establish and operate their own limited liability companies with Norway as a company address. In the Centre Party, we hope that this is the beginning of an era when small enterprises, whether limited liability companies, limited liability cooperatives or unlimited liability traders, will receive increased positive attention in the future. Cooperatives with limited financial liability, with an economic activity similar to that of limited liability companies, should be treated in the same way as limited liability companies. It is not part of the existing legislative proposals. In the view of the Centre Party, this is necessary because limited-liability cooperatives are as transparent a form of company as the limited-liability companies. The differences are only that the holdings have different names – share versus share. The Ministry of Finance has clarified in the legal work that one will follow this simplification case carefully in the future. As part of this, the Ministry of Finance must pay particular attention to both limited-liability and foundations. The requirement for economic activity must be similar to that of the limited liability companies exempted from the audit obligation.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e742	There is a great deal to be enjoyed in Norway, and there is also reason to rejoice in the decision to be taken later today by the Stortinget regarding the audit obligation — and it has also been underlined by several speakers, who have expressed the same joy. This is a proposal that has several balances. One is the relationship to simplification for the companies involved. There is a balance between control, information, supervision and the fact that, as far as the cards of the people on the other hand are concerned, but at the same time there is every reason to stress that this is in itself a great declaration of trust for the companies concerned. Then I also want to stress what was said in a previous line exchange, that it is not that one should not have an audit. Revision is important. In addition to the job to be done under the law, audit is also an important contributor to having good control procedures, to having good systems in each enterprise to ensure that one does so properly and in a good way. What is also happening is that the proposal implies that the framework conditions also for the smallest companies are adapted to international developments. Most EU and EEA countries have already granted exemptions from or have never had an audit obligation for the smallest limited liability companies. This also means strengthening the competitive situation of Norwegian companies. When you get a right to do something, it is often that you also have a duty, and one of the duties attached to the proposal that is now there is to provide extended information to the Brønn Island Health Registers, for example. in relation to the authorised accounting officer and how the accounts of individual companies are kept. Then, more people in the debate have argued that the issue has an important aspect to do with tax control issues. It is important that someone takes this into account, so that tax and tax controls have the means to control and combat financial crime. It also means that tax and charging authorities are allowed to impose audit obligations on public limited liability companies that have not been audited and the NUF with limited liability that is exempt from audit obligations for up to three years if detailed conditions are met. The solution now selected will be evaluated after some time, for example. in relation to whether there have actually been negative effects on tax control and financial crime. Then I would like to comment on some of the questions that have been raised in the vote, which have also been addressed in the debate so far. A relationship is a review of special certification requirements. The call for special certification requirements to assess whether the control profit exceeds the costs for business will now be reflected in the initiative of the Ministry of Finance towards other departments, so that they can review matters requiring certification, including: to grant grants. Individual ministries will be asked to review their grant schemes in order to assess the type of control measures necessary to impose on companies. Then it's about auditing obligations for parent companies in the group. I believe that this proposal presupposes that natural persons should be treated in the same way as group parent companies if they have controlling interests in several companies. Such a rule could help to prevent the possibility of circumventing the audit requirement by having companies in their own right instead of exercising ownership through a holding company. Such a rule would, in practice, have to include, in order to be effective, persons who do not currently have general accounting obligations under the Accounting Act. A prerequisite for a proposal which may give rise to an audit obligation in such cases is therefore that the accounting obligation and its detailed content are clarified for such persons, where appropriate, that audit obligations be imposed directly on companies in which those persons have ownership. Both options require investigation, and I see it as natural that in the evaluation you have notified, one goes through this question and returns to the Stortinget with the result of the evaluation. The number of companies involved has also been a subject of debate so far. According to the Central Bureau of Statistics, a total of over 136,000 non-financial limited liability companies meet all three thresholds. Below it is approx. 17 000 parent companies that will not be able to opt out of the audit, and the number of AS that the bill can deselect from the audit will be approximately 119 000. In addition, it is approx. 25 000 pure subsidiaries which may end up having to carry out certain audit acts to a greater or lesser extent because the parent company's accounts have to be audited. The issue of audit obligations for partnerships has also been a topic. We evaluated this in the proposition, but the short story here is that we are also going to review this in the evaluation in the light of the debate we have had today. I can stress that simplification is going to continue, and if the department finds that this is feasible in a good way, we will, of course, do it. ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e1225	First, I would like to commend the proposers for taking a positive initiative and sketching out possible solutions in an area in which the whole committee is involved. The Committee agrees with the proposers that the financial crisis has had historical proportions and that the crisis was the strongest in the world economy since World War II. The financial crisis also showed us how vulnerable banks are to disruptions in the money and capital markets, and that problems in the banking sector can easily have a negative impact on the economy in general. The financial crisis was met by strong global crisis packages. In Norway, government helped to mitigate the crisis through an expansive monetary and financial policy that maintained demand for goods and services. And Norway managed very well through the financial crisis with a small decline in production and a small increase in unemployment. We cannot say if many other countries – the financial crisis was followed by a debt crisis in those countries that had saved the financial sector through powerful crisis packages. Still, there is great uncertainty and uncertainty about the economy of many countries, and we see almost every day examples of how ordinary people now pay a high price for the rescue operation through unemployment, poorer welfare services and a lower private economy. We therefore see the need for strict international regulation of financial markets, so that crises do not occur. Norway has a regulation of financial markets that proved to be very favourable in the context of the international financial crisis. But we have an open and small economy, and it is therefore crucial for our interests that we have solid financial systems globally. The Committee points out that there is a major international effort to develop good enough regulation and supervision legislation for banks. Work focuses on the development of standards and recommendations on capital, liquidity and supervision requirements. Work carried out internationally will have an impact on Norway by incorporating amendments to EU financial legislation into the EEA Agreement. It is therefore essential that Norway contribute to the work on new rules, so that our experience of financial market regulation is heard when new international legislation is to be developed. The Committee has registered that regulatory changes have already been initiated in the Banking Act and in the case of information measures. We also received the end of the Financial Crisis Committee on 25 January, which proposes a number of measures to further develop financial market regulation in Norway. The Committee makes an important contribution to understanding the international financial crisis and the results of the crisis in Norway. It underlines the importance of keeping the economy in order and the importance of sound regulation, strong supervision and international cooperation in order to avoid new crises in the financial sector. Among other things, the sample highlights increased Nordic cooperation to regulate the financial market, including cooperation on stricter capital and liquidity requirements vis-à-vis banks, and specific requirements for systemic financial institutions. The Committee also proposes that the tax system should complement financial market regulation and contribute to more financial stability. In this context, the sample proposes the introduction of a tax — activity tax — on the profits and wages of financial institutions in order to tax added value generated in the financial sector. The selection is also concerned with consumer protection in the financial market. I think that is very positive, because it is sometimes difficult for consumers to understand the products offered. The Minister of Finance, when presenting the selection, said that he would follow the sample quickly and that the various measures would be assessed. We have also registered that the Minister of Finance in his letter to the Committee says that the proposals of the Christian People's Party take on board elements that are discussed both nationally and internationally. The majority of the Committee points out that, in the light of the work being done at national and international level, proposals for changes will be made in the usual way to secure us against a new financial crisis. The proposals of the Christian People's Party will be one of several inputs in this context, and the majority propose that the proposals be annexed to the Protocol.

It is therefore important to keep a constant focus on regulation of the sector, but it should not be forgotten that new regulation can cost in terms of efficiency. Unilateral regulation by Norway may have unintended effects in terms of weakening the ability of Norwegian banks to compete with their foreign competitors, thus increasing the risk of more funding for Norwegian value creation being transferred to operators and markets with lower regulatory requirements.’ This is precisely why the Progress Party believes it is right that we see the proposals of the Christian People's Party in the context of what is now being done in Europe and in the world, also linked to the proposals of the Committee on Finance, before we finally take a position on the possible new needs for new regulation in the wake of the financial crisis. One of the most central lessons of the financial crisis and one of the most important proposals we are making today is that the behaviour of banks needs to be better adapted to the risks involved. If the banks think they will be saved by the authorities if it goes wrong, it will also affect their willingness to perhaps take too much risk. In order to avoid such a risk, crisis resolution systems must ensure that those who have contributed to the risk-taking must be those who also bear the losses, not the inhabitants or the country. As a result, banks will also take less risk. The likelihood of bubble formation in the economy, with subsequent crises, will be reduced accordingly. The Basel Committee will introduce higher capital and liquidity requirements in order to improve the capacity of banks to face new crises. In order to prevent banks from tightening up their lending while growth is weak in many countries, this shall be gradual from 2013 to 2019. It's a long time to wait, said Governor Øystein Olsen in his recent year, as the banks in this country are going well. He therefore believed, as in one of the proposals we are making today, that we, together with our Nordic neighbours, should go ahead and introduce stricter rules. After all, Norway’s strict banking legislation has proved to be a strength, not a weakness, for the same banks. The high socio-economic and social costs of financial crises and the need to limit excessive risk-taking also make it appropriate to look at how we tax the financial industry. The International Monetary Fund, the IMF, in its report to the G20 Group, recently proposes a separate tax on the sum of profits and salaries of financial institutions. This we think is a proposal it is well worth looking at. The rest of business has to pay VAT, while a relatively strong financial industry has VAT exemptions. It's a paradox. We can look at an activity tax on profits and wages in the financial industry to compensate for this situation, also to ensure that the industry does not become too large and does not take too much risk. I can add as a small suggestion: we are discussing the tax on wealth right as it is in this Chamber. We did that in one of the previous debates. Maybe we'll look at switching such a tax with the wealth tax on taxing the active ownership. I think that might be an interesting thought. ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e1355	First, thank you to the rapporteur for a very balanced presentation of this matter. And this is not least because very many political regulation of the banking and financial industries were already in place. So, of course, the question is, is there something we can learn from the crisis? Is there a need for unilateral action in Norway to be better rusted next time? One of the key prerequisites for a well-functioning and dynamic market economy is that overall economic policy is stabilising over time. It is not possible, nor desirable, to try to remove all economic movements. It's normal for the economy to fluctuate slightly as a result of normal cyclical waves. The point of a stabilising policy is primarily to mitigate the most serious fluctuations and, in particular, to reduce the risk of deep and long-term crises. I want to point out some principles that are important to the Left. Firstly, it is important that the mandate of the Bank of Norway can also involve monetary policy measures that can counteract strong speculative bubbles associated with securities, dwellings and properties. Secondly, regulation of banks and the soundness and ability of other financial corporations to withstand cyclical fluctuations must be sufficiently robust and comprehensive and should be designed to countercyclical and thereby stabilise over time. Thirdly, budgetary policy must be based on the rule of action, which will help to create a predictable fiscal policy. In this way, the country’s financial robustness is also supported. Fourth, Norway must actively contribute to the building and further development of key institutions in international trade, financial and monetary systems, which are legitimate and effective, as many have experienced here today. Finally, open markets are best ensured through fair playing rules, neutral regulation and the freest possible competition at local, national and international level. It is important to be leading was and not believe that we have so much money on the book that we in any case solve future crises. In this way, this proposal by the Christian People's Party is an important reminder. Finally, a small visit to the representative of Thorkildsen. It is not that if one imposes a VAT-like tax system on the financial sector, it automatically means that it contributes to the Community. This means that the service is becoming more expensive for the user, and costs are often passed on to the customer. We saw that, for example. the introduction of VAT on sport tickets.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e1467	This is probably the worst debate I've taken the initiative on – a very serious topic, with cruel stories, but also a theme that shows some of the best that can live in people, just getting a commitment and an opportunity to help people. We've seen an area of war over many years, with bottomless despair and how many women and children are affected. But we've also seen hope and commitment, and we've seen that countries have gone ahead with good examples. Last night, when I prepared for this interpellation debate, I saw a documentary on TV from Congo. It was found that since 1996, several wars have been waging in the Congo. The country has 30,000 soldiers in its national army. In addition, they have 50,000 soldiers in up to 60 different armed groups. Hundreds of thousands of women and girls have been raped. Several of the soldiers in the militia have systematically used rape as a weapon of war. One of the former soldiers who was part of the documentary, who in retrospect became violent with bad memories and torments after what he had been on, said himself that we were like beasts. That documentary showed in an excellent way what is going on in the mind of those who are doing the abuses. One of those who have noticed the war strategy on the body is three-year-old Furaha, whose name means joy. The night of October 16th, 2008, what is going to affect the rest of her life. Together with her mother and father, she was sleeping in a shed in the Mugungua refugee camp in eastern Congo. That's when the mother hears that soldiers enter the camp and enter their shed. The mother manages to escape screaming out into the woods, while the soldiers shoot wildly into the room where the family is lying. It's only after two hours that she's awake back. Then she finds her husband killed, and the child lies in a blood pool. The child's womb is completely destroyed. The rest of the night the mother tries to sleep next to her dead husband and with three-year-old Furaha, who bleeds from the abdomen, on the other side. Early the next morning, the mother leaves her dead husband and takes her daughter on a 20-kilometre journey to the city of Goma and the women's centre, where she and the child can get help. The trip must take them on foot through the forest, where it is crawling with guerrillas. Well promoted at the women's centre of Mama Jeanette, which is supported by the Norwegian U.D. and the Norwegian organisation CRN, the child gets final help. Vesle Furaha is far from alone to be raped in the eastern Congo. This is a serious story – a terrible, cruel story. Anne Kristin Sydnes, head of foreign affairs in the Church's Emergency Aid, says the following: ‘Peace is necessary to bring an end to the rapes. But while we are working for peace, systematic protection of the civilian population must be given priority. Massive, collective rapes, where women's remains are being destroyed, are being used as weapons. The armed groups use the exodus, among other things, to scare people away from territories and territories rich in minerals. If the mineral trade is brought under control, we can have peace. The world cannot afford to give up.’ Congo is a country that is very rich in minerals that we use in mobile phones, PCs, DVD players and other electronics that we surround ourselves with every day. Part of the warring groups in the Congo and neighbouring countries finance their warfare by extracting and selling these minerals, and thus there is a link between the mineral wealth and the suffering suffered by women and children. Several documentaries have shown just this context in recent years. My interpellation is therefore about what we can do nationally and internationally, but not least about what we can do as consumers, and in particular the company's own social responsibility. Last year, more from the Stortinget were visiting the United Nations. There we met, among others. The new UN guard for Resolution 1325, Margot Wallström. It was a strong meeting with gloomy undertones, but also a meeting marked by hope. There are no simple solutions to such a comprehensive problem as what we see in Congo. Wide-ranging efforts are required in several fields, and not least international communities are required. The problems associated with mineral deposits and the numerous rapes are primarily a symptom of an even more extensive problem in the wake of the many wars that have led the Congo. The success of Norway at the top of the world over the best countries to live in is mainly due to the ability to ensure that community values are managed by the community and benefit the community. We want the same thing to happen with regard to mineral deposits in other countries, including Congo. Therefore, we must be very careful when looking for measures to stop the terrible situation of many Congolese people. In fact, it can quickly be that good things are best done to the enemy, that actions that are tempting – because then we show action – are proving to exacerbate the situation for many. In a recent report, funded by the UK authorities, Nicholas Garrett from Resource Consulting Services shows that such measures as bans and boycotts will be of little use and directly harmful. Garrett believes that a stop or a break in trade with tin and coltan will hit the weakest and could lead to a further escalation of the conflict. He wants a more proactive work, to gradually gain more official control over the illegal part of the mineral trade. In that case, all parties must be encouraged to participate and not withdraw. In fact, it is mainly a lack of governance in the Eastern Congo that allows local military groups to exploit the local population in the way we see it. First of all, they must put in place a well-functioning steering system in the Congo. In the documentary I was watching yesterday, they showed how big a challenge it is to assemble and build a serious, well-functioning national army. Many of the soldiers come from just armed groups and have been involved in rapes. Some men in fact stated that they would not be plagued with such questions as the army would disclose its work against rape. Congo, Rwanda and Uganda should be forced to take control of and disarm their militias. The UN should strengthen the mandate of its presence in order to protect the civilian population. The EU and the United States should contribute to the financing of the construction and training of a police force with a high proportion of women who can protect the civilian population. I want to pay great tribute to the volunteer organizations CARE, the Refugee Aid, the Church's Emergency Aid and the Joint Council for Africa, etc., who have worked for many years to better monitor the origins of minerals used in Western consumer goods. They want international tracking mechanisms for minerals, especially coltan, as they believe that this must include companies that buy and further develop coltan. An international tracking mechanism with the UN at the head is absolutely necessary to achieve long-term peace in the country, says CARE. We have seen in the past, in the so-called Kimberley process, that legislation was put in place to break the link between trade in rough diamonds and armed conflicts, just after pressure from grassroots and non-governmental organisations across borders. We should be able to get something similar here. The United States has shown that it's possible to do something. The UN contributes, and we need more countries that go ahead. The Norwegian Government is already playing a crucial role in the issue of global mineral management through the support to the EITI. The EITI is working for more transparency and transparency in the mining and oil industry, including in the Congo. Norwegian organisations can play a crucial role in lobbying Western governments to improve public structures and reform of the security sector in Congo. We want Norway’s objective to be to put in place international rules and standards that ensure transparency and secure mechanisms for the detection of conflict minerals, and that Norway cooperates closely with, among others. The United States and the EU on this issue. The United States is about to have rules in place, and the EU is now assessing rules in the same direction. We also want us to work for a national regime in this area while we are working to put in place international legislation, and I want the government to look further at it. But as important as what we can do nationally and internationally, it is to point to the responsibility that we all have, especially the manufacturers of electronic products. I myself was not aware that the mobile phone I carry could contain minerals with such a story. I don't get an answer to that if I ask. I think that is reprehensible. They control mining and keep it going as a pure military force, without any of what is called political goals. So you can't underestimate the background step for the interpellant taking up. Norway is a small country in Congo. It is next to Burma the largest country where Norway does not have an embassy. We're doing a good job trying to preserve the rainforests in Congo. One of them was to ensure that listed companies should publish where the minerals they use originate. It is not a boycott of the Congo, but it is a requirement that these companies have a responsible and open trade chain. We will now work closely with the Americans, including with the EU, to look at the possibilities of implementing similar legislation in Norway. Let me stress again that what we have to do has to be open, transparent. There has to be responsible business on the part of companies, and we have to do what we can to ensure that the military groups do not get control of this business. We presented a big message about corporate social responsibility last year, which brings up the responsibility of the Norwegian business community in such situations. We have been a driver of a series of international initiatives. The interpellant was involved in the so-called Extractive Industries Transparency Initiative. It is one of many important initiatives to ensure transparency around oil and gas and around mining activities. We are working on various initiatives in the OECD. So again, thank you to the interpellant for an important debate.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e1564	My starting point in this interpellation is based on the idea: think globally, act locally! The rapes are a cruel means, breaking up families and destroying communities, for the enrichment of the warlords in the country. The traumatisation of civil society is thus a grotesque part of the resource use in many of Congo's mines. So you can ask yourself how this is possible. I don't have all the answers to that, but I'll nevertheless venture on some considerations around it. If you put it a little bit on the verge, you could say that capitalism and the back of the market promote such a oppressive system. The demand for these goods thus enables this resource struggle. A simple causal solution may be that a stop to the demand for these minerals also removes the basis for the struggle for resources, which in turn put an end to the abuses on the civilians. But I don't think this is the magic formula for peace and reconciliation alone in Congo. This may also involve ethnic conflicts, which are also part of a complex conflict picture of contradictions and violence. The market for these minerals is large, and therefore, in my view, the capital that buys these commodities has a responsibility to remedy the situation. The sale of these minerals to various international companies that produce gold rings, cell phones, etc. helps to finance weapons and medicines for military forces in Congo, funds that in turn enable the abuses we see today. It is with this background that I believe that the use of capital must reverse this development. Capitalism and the allocation of capital are a good instrument for promoting peace and development. Norway is a good example of this, where our natural resources have laid the foundation for the development we have had of civil society. We can also contribute to this in Congo. Apple and Nokia. As consumers, we should be responsible for the fact that the mobile phones we buy are not produced with metals from conflict mines. We can ask international companies that sell their products in Norway to document where the minerals in e.g. The PC is coming from. So we can also make sure that what we buy is not part of financing the violence in Congo. At the same time, through an active consumer policy, we can also say no to the products that come from conflict mines. But we can also introduce the mines that operate legally and that contribute to the positive development of civil society in Congo. That way, we give incentives to change the situation locally in Congo. We are then using the market and capital to promote positive development in Congo. In summary, I would like to point out what I said in the introduction – that we can change the situation in Congo by acting locally. Our consumer voice can change everyday life and create a better everyday life for those who are exploited, by showing international solidarity locally.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e1585	Let me also thank the representative Stokkan-Grande for taking this serious and difficult matter up in Stortinget today. Conflict minerals are minerals extracted in a situation characterized by armed conflict and human rights violations, and in this case they are the so-called Democratic Republic of Congo, which I am now pleased to call me Congo. The minerals are partly extracted under the protection of forces belonging to the Congolese army, but also under the protection of or by a number of rebel groups which are partly in combat against each other. This helps to maintain the conflict in the country and the suffering of the local population. In many places, armed groups use extortion, forced recruitment and violence to control the movement of minerals. In Congo, there are huge natural resources. It is estimated that about 80 pst. of the occurrence of coltan in Congo, and 49 pst. of global cobalt resources and 10 pst. of the world's deposits of copper are to be found in Congo. In addition, there have been large deposits of gold and diamonds, and the struggle for these resources is merciless. In a world historical perspective, only the two world wars are passing over the war in Congo. More than 5.4 million people have been killed in the conflict since 1996, either as a direct consequence of armed acts or as a result of rape, assault, disease and famine. About 1 million people have become refugees in this conflict. But something is being done internationally to find ways to reduce the use of violence around mineral extraction. There are both legally binding legislation and voluntary mechanisms that regulate trade in and trade in conflict minerals. Internationally, the US Dodd-Frank Act on Conflict Minerals is, as the Council of State was also involved. This includes all companies reporting to the United States Securities and Exchange Commission. The law was signed by President Obama on 21 July last year, and it is expected that the final regulations will be in place during April – this month – this year. This includes minerals originating from, or allegedly originating from, the Congo and nine countries nearby and includes reporting obligations for companies marketing such minerals. Other binding guidelines in this area are United Nations Security Council Resolution 1952 of 29 November last year, which extends, inter alia: The arms embargo against the Congo. This concerns the OECD Due Diligence Guidance and the Transparency initiative in the extractive industries – EITI – which was also the subject of the interpellant, which are voluntary mechanisms to provide ethical guidance for the industry. Congo is actually a candidate country for the EITI, and the EITI Governing Board stated last December that the Congo is close to meeting the membership requirements. The UK Government is working with international partners to help bring the natural resources of the Congo under legitimate democratic control so that resources can contribute to tax revenue to the Congolese state. The EU assesses the possibility of introducing the same requirements and guidelines as the United States under the so-called Dodd-Frank legislation. I had hoped that the Council of State here today could say that Norway is choosing to join the EU in this process. Here, extractive companies listed on American exchanges must publish what they pay to the countries concerned for the extraction and marketing of minerals. The extraction of conflict minerals in Congo is difficult to control. Another challenge is that measures must include not only the Congo but also neighbouring countries. In the Kimberley process involving conflict diamonds, it is possible to trace the chemical structure of individual diamonds. Unfortunately, this is not so easy for other materials, but a work is being done, not least in the Federal Institute of Earth Science and Natural Resources of Germany, to find techniques for creating so-called fingerprinting systems to identify minerals. But this is time- and cost-intensive. Therefore, the question put by Mr Stokkan-Grande is very relevant. There's a lot going on in this field in the world, but there's a long way left.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e1607	In the novel 'Heart of Darkness', by Polish-British writer Joseph Conrad, published in 1899, the author depicts some of the enormous abuses that took place in the Congo. The novel was strongly inspired by Conrad's own experience as a riverboat captain in Congo. Many today probably know better about the story of the 1979 film "Apocalypse Now!", where the script was based on "Heart of Darkness", but the story moved to the Vietnam War. Joseph Conrad's novel greatly contributed to raising the international opinion that led to the official Belgian takeover of the area. From 1885 until 1908, the Democratic Republic of Congo was ruled by King Leopold II of Belgium. The country then had the famous name Free State Congo, which otherwise suited very well to the enormous brutality that was used and demonstrated. The Private State of Congo was based on slavery, torture, terror and exchange. After the liberation of Belgian rule in 1960 and until the late 1970s, all the documents on King Leopold II and the mass murder of millions of people were secret. King Leopold himself justified despite all the acquisition of the Free State of Congo, for the rest of the world well to note, that the natives were to be civilized and that the slave trade that Muslims engaged in in Africa was to be eradicated. Under the leadership of Leopold, the local population suffered from a horror that forced them to collect raw materials, especially raw rubber, into the king's personal profit. The Democratic Republic of Congo gained its independence from Belgium in 1960, after the national elections were held in May of that year. The centre party NCE won the election, and Patrice Lumumba became the country's first democratically elected prime minister. After independence, an uprising immediately broke out in the army, and the mineral-rich province of Katanga was attempted to detach. The following year, Prime Minister Patrice Lumumba was executed by troops loyal to military commander Joseph Mobutu. In 1965 Mobutu took power, changed the name of the country to Zaire and took the name Mobutu Sese Sesko himself. While Lumumba was a nationalist and wanted to nationalize the mines, Mobutu Zaire turned into a stepping stone for operations against Soviet-supported Angola. He was rewarded with American support throughout the Cold War. Zaire himself became synonymous with corruption. Rarely has a state been so systematically plundered by its own leaders. Let me mention two other historically important events closer to the present. After the 1994 Rwandan genocide, a significant number of those responsible for the killings escaped at about 800000 ethnic tussiers and Hutu across the border of the Democratic Republic of Congo. Rwandan-supported insurgents cast President Mobutu Sese Seko after Congoese Interahamwe insurgents repeatedly attacked Rwanda from Congo. Laurent Kabila was appointed as a new leader, and the country was renamed Congo. That conflict was named the first war in Congo. The second war in Congo lasted from 1998 until a peace agreement was signed four years later. During this period the UN accused several high-ranking political and military officials from Rwanda, Uganda and Zimbabwe of exploiting their intervention in the Democratic Republic of Congo to plunder the huge mineral wealth there, especially diamonds. Just since 1998, about 5.4 million people have died. So too many people are still on the run because there are still new games. The most important export products in the Congo today are copper, diamonds, crude oil, coffee and rubber. This is also the essence of the country’s close history. People, both Europeans, locals, Americans and now Chinese people are trying — and have tried — to make themselves rich in the exploitation of natural resources. Many have done so, but every single time, the civilian population has been severely punished. This is largely a forgotten conflict. I took up the historical, just because history repeats itself. We must be able to learn from what has happened. It is commendable that the representative Stokkan-Grande takes this up, precisely because we have the opportunity to do something. Let me mention a couple of things. It is very important that the state of Congo has control over that entire Republic. It is very important that what comes out of the earth is taxed. It is very important that there be a judicial settlement after the genocide in Congo, and the military must exit, the militias must be under national control. The development of minerals such as coltan and others must take place in transparency.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e1691	Motor travel in outland has been debated repeatedly in recent years, based on representative proposals from different parties. The Ministry’s lack of capacity or willingness to intervene in this matter is clearly demonstrated by the fact that the pilot project is still under way, ten to eleven years after its launch. In its reply to the Committee, the Ministry of the Environment refers to the Directorate of Natural Management's recommendation. In view of this, in June 2009 the government implemented some changes to the regulation for the use of motor vehicles in the open air and on the covered watercourses. Among other things, the use of motor vehicles in the transport of materials and equipment for off-road use was extended to group trips with snowmobiles for health and care establishments, etc. and for municipal emergency measures to reduce the risk of impact on elk and other deer by road or rail. The multi-territorial political platform states that the government will complete the review of the law on motor travel in the fields and watercourses. This means that the government will assess the need for further adjustments or changes to existing legislation, in addition to what was implemented in 2009. There are two main issues that remain to be resolved: whether to open up for recreational driving south of North Troms and whether to establish a home in motorcycling in order to withdraw the local authorities’ possibility of exemption. Fortunately, the NINA reports can indicate a number of positive effects of the test project. In many municipalities, there has been less illegal driving, more taxi and tourism, less driving in the most vulnerable areas, better municipal planning and a stronger internal justice system in the scooter environment. The municipalities involved in the project have been able to establish both tourist loops and recreational loops. This has meant that both visitors and locals have had the opportunity to get into the field. In the case of a very controversial barrage, there is a broad political consensus that a very restrictive line exists there, which I feel is also the basis for the municipalities to avoid damage to nature. I myself visited the municipality of Fauske, which is one of the municipalities that have established a leopard, which is located in Sulitjelma. The recreational loop system has enabled the elderly and the disabled to get into the wild even in winter. Similarly, tourism businesses in municipalities have been able to offer their guests natural experiences on snowmobiles, as is possible for all Swedish and Finnish travel businesses. The test system was described as a clear success, and there were clear expectations that the system was permanent. Local management through planning and building laws works well and is a good and appropriate tool to use for motor travel in outland. In this way, politicians and residents become conscious of their use of land and nature, so that one avoids motor travel where nature is vulnerable, or where one does not want such movement. The Progress Party believes that all municipalities should be given the right to manage motorised travel in the outland by means of planning and building laws. This will increase the willingness of municipalities to carry out good municipal planning and monitoring of the most vulnerable natural areas, and will be able to take good care of all the interests associated with the management of motor travel in rural areas. The Progress Party also has proposals for legislative changes through Document 8:79 L for 2009-2010. The suggestions contained in this document are based on the positive experience gained from the pilot project. By basing these amendments, the government can save time and energy on further spending, and instead end the matter of joy for the Norwegian people, who are busy getting into nature, also through the use of snowmobiles. This also has a clear public health perspective. Finally, I will deal with the proposals of the Progress Party. I mean, it's time to put this thing in place right now. Subsidy, we support the Right's somewhat more bureaucratic and time-consuming proposals.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e1720	Noise, damage to nature, disruption of animal life and potential conflicts with outdoor interests are the potential consequences of motor travel in the outland. Motor travel in outland is prohibited today, unless there are specific exceptions. It is clear that the use of motor vehicles in outdoors has positive aspects as well, and serves good purposes, among other things. in the reindeer industry and in other management of land. In addition, it is important for police and rescue services. It is important to have a balance between these considerations. In Soria Moria II, the government writes that it will complete the review of the law on motor travel in the fields and watercourses. Based on this review, it shall be assessed whether there is a need for further adjustments or amendments to existing legislation in addition to that implemented in the summer of 2009. In 2009, we amended the regulations on access to business, group trips with snowmobiles and municipal emergency measures to reduce the risk of impact on elk and other deer. The Ministry of the Environment is now reviewing the issues that remain in the Directorate of Natural Management’s 2008 recommendation. I am concerned that we should find a solution that helps to balance the needs of certain groups for the use of motor vehicles in the field and the protection of nature and outdoors. Motor travel in the outfield is controversial. However, I note that several surveys between 2001 and now show that a clear majority of the population is against more liberal legislation in this area. The study highlights the particular importance of outdoor life as a reason for a restrictive approach. A significantly more liberal approach to this could give the interests of a minority priority over the interests of the majority. I also record that the Progress Party refers to a study on these conditions and that this study has been carried out in the seven most scooter-rich states of our country. The Ministry of the Environment launched a pilot scheme, on which the rapporteur was involved, with new management of motor traffic in selected municipalities in 2000. The municipalities of Sirdal, Vinje, Stor-Elvdal, Lom, Røros, Hattfjelldal, Fauske and Kautokeino were part of the scheme. Kautokeino later withdrew from the scheme. In these attempts, more administrative powers were transferred to the municipalities. As the rapporteur says, good results from the experiment have been demonstrated, but these are not quite clear results either. The evaluation of the results will be part of the government's further work on the matter. In this case, it has been important for the Workers' Party to find a good balance between the need for motor travel in the field and the need for outdoor life and the preservation of the landscape. We will also take this into account when we discuss the matter further when the government comes up with its evaluation.

We all need natural experiences, and we should all have rights in this context, but in a way that preserves both nature and those who seek peace in nature, whether it is in the outland or in the spring.

The pilot project with a new management system for outdoor motor travel in eight municipalities started in 2000 and was evaluated by the Norwegian Institute for Natural Research (NINA) in 2005. The evaluation was the basis for the NINA proposal for a new legislation. In my home state, West Agder, Sirdal has been an experimental community. The municipality developed in 2002 a plan for motor travel in rural areas. Sirdal municipality is a very large cottage municipality. Sirdal's politicians stressed in the preparation of the plan that most of the driving was based on commercial activity. The plan was to work for three years and be renewed annually. Now the plan was made in 2002, until further notice. Based on the experience of the pilot project, the politicians in Sirdal want to revise the plan, including: areas for recreational use.
