ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e2442	Several have not asked for the floor on case No. 11th@item: inlistbox [[Fotation, see page 3376]]
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e2458	In Norway, a high level of confidence is attached to the correct implementation of the choice. The implementation of choices requires the establishment of a comprehensive apparatus in each municipality, with many detailed procedures in which all electoral officials must intervene in order to ensure the correct implementation of the choice. The choice is carried out every two years, and that means that it is not a routine task, which poses challenges for ensuring continuity. In Norway, we trust that this will be done in a proper way, and then it is our responsibility as politicians to regulate the electoral law when we now see that it is necessary to maintain the good trust we have in the Norwegian election implementation. In spring 2009, the law on electoral elections introduced a new provision for observation of elections. According to § 15-10, the Department has the right to accredited election observers upon application. The OSCE conducted a limited election observation at the 2009 elections. Following the election, the Ministry has evaluated the implementation of the elections by carrying out a wide range of surveys in municipalities and county municipalities. The OSCE pointed to some aspects and asked the Norwegian electoral authorities to assess changes in legislation or procedures, and so we have done so. In the light of the feedback and the ministry's evaluation of the elections, the Ministry of Local and Regional Affairs of Prop. 64 L proposals for certain changes to the law on elections, and the following amendments have been proposed: An obligation for the public register to establish a provisional number on 1 April, based on the voting conditions, for the control of electoral lists. This is Section 2-5 of the Election Act. A right to ask for exemption from making a choice. Requirements for list candidates not to be able to serve as voting recipients or electoral officials. Requirements for the sealing of ballot boxes during voting. Saturdays shall be treated as public holidays for the purposes of calculating the time limits laid down by law. At the same time, the proposal for the following amendment to the municipal law is promoted: the requirement that list candidates are not eligible for vote. The Committee has examined these proposals, and I will go a little further into the various proposals. First of all, it is clear from the election law that the public register authority is to make available to the electoral authorities information on who is to be introduced into the municipal population. This implies that the public register sets the date of intersection as soon as possible, which is the date on which the municipality in which the voter is voting is located, and shall make the number of votes available to the municipalities. The date of entry into force of the electoral law is 30 June of the year of the election. The responsibility also implies that, after the date of the cut-off and until the date of the election, the public register authority shall make available to the municipalities the changes in the population register which affect the entry and printout of the number of persons, because a new number is created for each election. A large majority of the hearing bodies support the proposal for a provisional number to be assigned on 1 April of the electoral year for the purpose of verifying candidates and signatures by the county councils. The Committee has looked at this and cannot see that prior access to provisional ratings will have an impact on the printing of ballots, as long as the time limit for approval is the same, namely 1 June. The 2009 election evaluation shows that the county electoral councils want an extraction of the population register as early as possible, but at the same time there should be a minimum difference in relation to the normal population produced on 30 June of the electoral year. We support that amendment, that is to say, an overall committee. The next thing I'm going to go into is the obligation to make a choice, which is also referred to as the ombudsman's obligation. It is governed by the Constitution and the Election Act. The ombudsman's obligation is the principle that the voter can also be chosen without having given his consent. It's "without having given his consent" that we've looked at a little bit. There are some reasons for exemption which make it possible to claim exemption from having to appear on an electoral list. In the case of roll-call elections, one can demand exemption if one has voting rights in another state, has met as a representative of all the magnifications since the previous elections, or is a member of another political party. In order to require exemption from the requirement to be on an electoral list in county elections and municipal elections, one must have been a member of the competent body for the last four years, be entitled to exemption under other laws, be a member of a political party other than the one on which it is listed, or be unable to discharge duties without disproportionate difficulty. Following its observation of the 2009 Grand elections, as I mentioned earlier, the OSCE requested that the relationship between human rights and that part of the ombudsman obligation entailing an obligation to be on an electoral list should be examined. It was in particular the fact that membership of other political parties allowed the OSCE to ask questions about whether this was in line with the principle of freedom of association, because freedom of association also requires freedom of association. So if you don't want to be a member anywhere, you have to be free to let it go. It is out of the question if the citizens feel that they must join a party with which they do not agree, in order not to be elected as representatives of a party with which they do not agree. The exemption rules for wholesale choice are laid down in the Basic Law. Thus, the provisions on the ombudsman obligation in the event of a rollover cannot be amended without a constitutional change first. In the case of local and regional elections, the provisions may be amended by the electoral law and that is what is now proposed. This was justified on the part of the Ministry by the fact that the real need for exemptions from the ombudsman obligation is greater in local government and county elections than in decision-making. There are also few or no comparable countries that have such a statutory obligation, and it is difficult to point out that there is a special need for such rules here that other countries do not have. Although the ombudsman has a long history in Norway, society has also changed a lot. The fact that everyone has a responsibility for the community and that participation is a civic duty is an important element of our democracy. There may therefore be good reasons for maintaining the ombudsman’s obligation, albeit in a slightly narrower form. It is therefore proposed that it should be sufficient for a person to submit in writing to the electoral authorities that they declare that they do not wish to be included in the list in question in order to obtain an exemption. It is that you must not express or deny political views. However, active action is still required to be exempted within a specified time limit, and there is an overall committee that considers this to be a good assessment. So over to the rules for time-limiting: The Ministry has proposed to us that the rules on time-limiting in the first, second and third paragraphs of Section 15 of the Act be amended. This implies that Saturdays are treated in the same way as public holidays with regard to the start or expiry of time limits under the law. Thus, the legal text is adjusted in relation to developments in society, where Saturdays are established as a day off for large groups of workers, both in public and private activities. The Committee agrees that Saturday is a holiday and supports this. So I'll talk a little bit about requirements for list candidates. After observing the decision-making process in autumn 2009, the OSCE took up the practice of using list candidates as members of the Electoral Board and voter. The OSCE noted that potential conflicts of interest may arise during the resolution of the elections when list candidates are involved. The minority of the Committee agrees with the assessment that persons who make their own choices should not at the same time have roles as voting recipients and election officials in a polling room. It is important that the elections are felt as neutral as possible, and the SV, the Central Party and the Workers' Party, which are the Committee's minority on this point, believe that it will strengthen this principle. Restrictions on the eligibility of the list candidates to voters are proposed. Based on the same assessments, similar restrictions are proposed for voting and electoral officials in the polling stations. The Ministry has stressed that only those who make their own choices in the current election, and not politicians in general, are subject to any restriction. In view of the fact that several municipalities believe that the restrictions will cause practical problems, the implementation of the proposal will be added to the 2013 elections. This will allow the municipalities sufficient time for conversion and for adapting to a new regulatory framework, as well as for a good transfer of competences. There are already several municipalities that want to mannounce voters without using list candidates. Until then, there will be contributions from the Ministry to strengthen this work. The Committee's minority believes that this will have a positive and inclusive effect on local democracy – that more people will be included in the electoral winding-up processes. If I know the majority of the committee right, they'll explain their own views later. So I'll talk a little bit about sealing election tournaments. With regard to the issue of sealing, storage and transport procedures for electoral material, something is regulated by the Election Act and something is regulated by the Election Regulation. The Committee considers that the proposal that the ballot boxes should be sealed also on the day of the election and in advance of the vote is such an important principle that it should be reflected in the electoral law itself. This has also received solid support in the consultation round. In this way, it is signalled that the requirement of the electoral law on the sealing of ballot boxes through all phases of the electoral process is fundamental and an important contribution to maintaining confidence in the impartiality of the electoral administration and that the elections are conducted with integrity. A Joint Committee supports this. Of course, it opens up for practical solutions. When the urn is full, the seal must be capable of being broken, etc., but it is important that good procedures are established for how this should be done in the event. The last thing I want to mention is a legal change in § 2-7 of the Election Act. It says the word ‘changeable’ instead of the word ‘written’. The entire Committee agrees with the Ministry that typographical errors should be corrected and therefore supports this amendment. I take this up with me the proposal of the Labour Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Central Party.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e2529	The representative Jette F. Christensen has taken up the suggestion she referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e2532	Let me first thank the President-in-Office for a good piece of work. She was thrown into this matter a little late because of changes in committee composition, but she has done a very good job and made a very good contribution and a good account of the Committee's view and treatment here now. As the rapporteur said, it is a unanimous committee that supports most of the amendments promoted by the government. However, there is one reservation and one clear disagreement. I want in my post to focus on the two things. The reservation is related to the changes to the ombudsman obligation, where the government of the proposition assumes that similar changes proposed in this proposition should also apply to grossing-up. Then it is a prerequisite for a constitutional change to be made. Neither the Progress Party nor the other opposition parties will address this issue now. Supporting some simplifications for practical reasons in the obligation to ombudsmen for local people does not automatically support similar changes for elections to the Stortinge. The great disunity – to the extent that it may be called – is whether or not to restrict the rights of citizens selectively for list candidates. Today, people who participate in democracy by making themselves available on electoral lists can also participate in the democratic processes of the electoral room. There they may participate in voting boards and may participate as electoral officials or as receivers of votes. The background to this scheme was probably first and foremost of practical importance. Those who participate in democracy by making choices also have insight into the processes of the electoral room. They are available, cheap in operation and concerned with the optimal implementation of the choice. Most people probably think of this as some kind of community service. But it also has important principal aspects because it is a democratic right to participate in democratic processes, including the democratic arenas that the constituencies make up. The majority of the committee believes that today's system is a good one, but the government and its parties in the Storting will therefore prohibit the participation of list candidates in these democratic processes. It is a somewhat strange approach, and the majority believe that there should be strong reasons for restricting democratic rights for citizens who, incidentally, wish to be listed candidates. In addition, there is no specific negative impact on that scheme, i.e. there are no disadvantages documented. The only thing the government says is from its own chest and to the extent that as a consequence of the OSCE report, it can theoretically weaken the voters' confidence in the electoral system if list candidates can still participate in democratic processes in the electoral room – despite the fact that there is no empire, nothing that supports this view. I think it is sad that the Storting will later today decide to restrict the democratic rights of list candidates against the will of the majority committee – not because this is necessarily a very big question in itself, of very great importance, but perhaps because the Storting once again drops a bit of the principle democracy debate and replaces it with a sort of peripheral debate on theoretical issues. This restriction also has the practical importance of making choices more expensive, in particular elections over two days, because many municipalities already use municipal staff with full rights to fill the polls as a complement to politicians both on and off the lists. Of course, if the policy entry is removed, or significantly reduced, the costs of implementing the elections will increase, and this may lead to fewer municipalities implementing elections over two days to save money at the expense of democratic participation. At the same time, several are likely to reduce the number of polling stations for the same reason. So that means that today's proposal could have consequences for participation in elections, and that would be very negative. That is probably also why the entire 33 municipalities have opposed this amendment, and only 14 are in favour. The Government is being implemented in the Storting for this restriction of the democratic rights of the list candidates by a limited majority. So they force them through the government's will, despite the fact that there is no obvious reason for the restriction. I think that is a pity, but majority rule is a consequence of democracy, even in terms of restricting democratic rights.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e2564	In Norway, however, it is important to maintain continuous follow-up and evaluation to ensure that this confidence is maintained. At the same time, it is known that system changes themselves can often contribute to an increase in the risk of errors. Therefore, it is important that we do not adopt changes just for the sake of change. One of the biggest changes in the setting is the change of § 3-4, which deals with a general exemption rule from selection to filling and local government. The OSCE, the Organisation for Security and Cooperation in Europe, has requested that the obligation to make elections be reviewed in order to ensure full compliance with the United Nations Convention on Civil and Political Rights. It states that no one should be forced to be a member of a political party or forced to state his political position. The ombudsman obligation has a long tradition in Norway, but at the same time it is a rather strange phenomenon. Today's legislation does not require consent to be nominated on an electoral list. To demand exemption, one must either have been in employment for the last four years, be able to prove that one is a member of another political party, or be unable to take care of the position without disproportionate difficulties. Today's practice, on the other hand, in my experience, is that all the list candidates are asked, and that a wish not to appear on the list is respected by all parties. In this way, we can say that today's practice is now being legalised. Another significant change is that list candidates for filling or local authorities are no longer eligible to vote as voters or may be voters or officials in the polling room. I notice that the opposition describes this as a reconstructed problem. In its evaluation, the OSCE writes: ‘In order to prevent actual or perceived conflicts of interest, or situations that may be perceived as conflicts of interest, the OSCE considers that list candidates should not be able to participate in the polling boards and electoral boards.’; On the way, I agree that this is not an actual problem in Norway, but with this change, list candidates will avoid feeling unwarranted suspicious. An electoral officer shall not only check the identity of the voters and receive the vote. He or she also has obligations to assist the election candidate in the election in case one wants it. Often this selector applies to people who have different degrees of dementia. I have many times felt uncomfortable to be asked to help find the right party list when I have been a candidate for the list. One realizes that other voters in the room may wonder if one is tempted to influence the selector. I would like to make it clear that there are only list candidates who cannot serve as officials in the polling rooms. Instead, it seemed that all politicians were excluded from being an electoral officer, but that's not the way it is. In my home municipality, there is a practice in which former politicians, former municipal councillors and local councillors who have not re-elected will continue to be eligible for these boards. I don't think there'll be a need for paid civil servants in all of these functions. I think there are still many politicians who are not on the electoral lists and who can fill these tasks as before. I would also like to make it clear that this does not apply to the choice of the harvest. The municipalities therefore have plenty of time to prepare. If that's the case for municipal elections only – we don't know that until it comes up as a constitutional case, if any – then they have four years to prepare for this change. Minimum opening time requirements in the constituencies have also been discussed. We have a huge gap in the number of voting rights per constituency here in Norway, actually from 4 people to over 10,000. So the need for adaptations to opening times and other adaptations will be very different. The centre party believes that the municipalities themselves will be best able to adapt both opening hours, choice of local and logistics in the room according to local needs and without new state requirements. On the other hand, the universal design requirement is, of course, a firm one. The selection room shall be accessible to all.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e2593	The case-speaker has done well for the proposals that have been made and for the Committee's position on the matter. Both the National Election Board and the Powers Committee have said, after previous elections, that they are positive for the development of a central administrative electoral data system. Such a system shall be tested in 10 municipalities at the option of the autumn. Ruling to all municipalities, maintenance, management and operation of such a system will require an extension of both technical and operational tasks in the future. I regret, therefore, that the Committee does not believe that a centrally independent electoral unit should be created here in the country – without the government being able to assess this and other alternatives more closely. All in all, this is a matter in which important questions of principle are discussed, and I am glad – as I said in the introduction – that the Committee essentially supports the proposals proposed by the government.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e2788	This case is about improving the local authorities' assumptions in order to provide a sufficient number of housing with lucky day care and health care facilities. I would like to further use the concept of care facilities for such dwellings and places for convenience. In the proposal, the Government proposes to increase the grant rates for care facilities with retroactive effect, as announced in Prop 29 S for 2010–2011. As part of the Committee’s examination of the matter, an open hearing was held on 15 February 2011 with the participation of several organisations. There is an overall committee, with the exception of the Progress Party, which is behind the Committee's recommendation for a decision. The grant for nursing homes was introduced to help municipalities with the promise that it could be to invest in a large number of nursing homes. The investment grant is a key instrument for achieving the objective of the Health Care Plan 2015. The measure has also been important for municipalities. Since 2008, a total of 4,047 places have been committed, but the amount of applications fell significantly during 2010, and all funds within the framework have not been used. The government has an ambitious goal of creating 12,000 care places. In order to achieve this objective, municipalities need to have frameworks and assumptions that enable them to do so. The task of the county councillors to prioritize the municipalities receiving grants has been terminated with effect from 2011 through Prop. 1 S for 2010–2011. At the same time, the distribution of frameworks between regions and counties was also lifted. The proposed change in grant rates goes from NOK 666 600 to NOK 888 800 in 2011, or an increase in the subsidy rate from 30 pst. to 40 pst. the costs of hospitalisation facilities. Similarly, an increase of 10 percentage points is proposed for housing with day-to-day care services. Many municipalities have a tradition of cooperating with housing teams or other ideal actors to provide care facilities. A review of the rules for the re-allocation of the grant from municipalities to such non-profit actors has therefore been carried out. Since the review shows that it is impossible to assess in advance who can receive the grant without breaching the State aid rules, municipalities have in practice been cut off from cooperating with other actors today. In order to remedy this situation, the government proposes to amend the rules so that the grant is paid to the municipality and the right to re-allocation is lost. The condition for payment to the municipality when the places are finished is that the places have the services of good luck, that there is detergent adaptation and universal design. In addition, municipalities must ensure a well-meaning right of assignment for 30 years, compared to 20 years today. Today, municipalities in Norway manage more than 18.5 million square metres of purpose building. The sector is one of the country’s largest property managers and is best placed to manage buildings in a good way. There is therefore a basis for pointing out that providing municipalities with flexible frameworks that ensure good welfare for citizens is important if we are to achieve the ambitious objectives. These changes shall ensure that municipalities can cooperate with NPISHs and housing teams without breaching the State aid rules. The accounting base of a building is written off over 30 years in this case. However, there is reason to point out that construction can have a real value in terms of either value in use, a technical value or a plot value even after 30 years. The town hall in Bodø, for example, which has stood for 50 years until next year, has definitely a value, even though it appears most often in the accounts as a cost item. For many local politicians, as for the majority, it is obvious that the municipality will in many cases be the most appropriate owner of the buildings in which welfare services are to be provided. It is not a goal to have as many people as possible living in a nursing home in Norway. On the contrary, it must be a goal that as little as possible live in nursing homes with good luck or in nursing homes. The aim must be to make it possible for as many people as possible to live in their own homes for as long as possible. But for those who need it, there should be provision of nursing homes, and the changes proposed in this case are an important step towards success. Equally important may be the interaction reform and the focus on prevention measures that lie there. If more people can be healthier, it will be the very best measure to provide adequate care homes. In the wake of the cooperation reform, I believe that this will be the basis for discussing how we can contribute to the other end of the scale. How can we ensure that measures, ranging from smaller home adjustments to larger conversions, can be taken so that more people can live longer in their own homes? We're going to get to the next crossroads. With this, I recommend the Committee's recommendation.

The background was that the potential contributor had set such a confirmation as a condition for providing its support. The action plan aims to ensure an overall approach to prevention, as well as to increase knowledge in this area, in order to ensure that prevention becomes more effective and targeted, and that prevention does not interfere or contribute to the opposite. With better cooperation between different actors at an early stage, we also facilitate broader and more effective prevention and thus increased security. Norway is also obliged to contribute to the fight against international terrorism, and both the Police Security Service and Ecocrime are involved in this work. Our international obligations have been followed up in national law, for example. Section 147b of the penal code, which directly targets the financing of acts of terrorism and the general responsibility for participation in Norwegian law, and which is a clear prohibition against criminal activities, not activities protected by human rights. We're gonna be happy for that. It is also important that efforts to prevent religious extremism ensure respect for the values and rights we want to protect. Freedom of belief and life is a fundamental human right, which means that the individual has the right to believe what he or she wants, to express and live in harmony with his or her faith, to change faith and to have a view of life that does not involve having a religious faith. This freedom also includes a collective right to come together on religious and life-seeking purposes, to participate in religious rituals together with others, and to organize a common religious life, enshrined in the Human Rights Convention, in the Norwegian law, in the religious communities, in the ymist others, and also in the Norwegian constitution. The government believes there is a need for increased transparency about large foreign gifts to religious and life-seeking communities and religious educational institutions in Norway. The Ministry of Culture will therefore investigate the establishment of a public register of gifts for such recipients.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e3543	This representative proposal from the Left was promoted in the wake of the Maria Amelie case and contains eight proposals which have as their common denominator to facilitate the situation of immigrants residing illegally in Norway. The Committee supports the aims of the proposers to minimise the illegality of Norway. The Committee also supports the views of the proposers on the need to seek voluntary return arrangements and the use of forced return where possible. In the proposal, proposers wish to focus on those who cannot be sent out of the country because Norway ‘does not have a return agreement with the country of origin, because the country of origin refuses to accept it, or because it is practically impossible, or very difficult, to return the persons’. The majority of the Committee, all except for the Christian People's Party, would point out that what is listed in the preceding paragraph is a heterogeneous group, most of which can return to their country of origin voluntarily. Furthermore, the majority would point out that there should be a difference between the residence assessment of persons cooperating with the authorities on return and identification and those who do not. The Committee further points out that the matter has been referred to the Minister of Justice, who, in his reply of 18 February, carries out a thorough review of the proposals. The Committee would point out that the proposers in the heading use the term ‘migrants in Norway without a legal stay’, while in the text they use the term ‘paperless’. The Committee will use the term ‘migrants in Norway’ in its opinion. ‘Proposal 1: The Storting asks for a human rights analysis of the situation of paperless persons in Norway’ To this end, the majority of the Committee considers that Norway has committed itself, through its accession to a number of international conventions, to take account of human rights and that it is assumed that the relationship with human rights is assessed by the introduction of legislation in all disciplines. On this basis, the majority do not wish to support the proposal. ‘Proposal 2: The Storting asks the Government to consider when granting permanent residence permits to long-term residents in Norway on the basis of an assessment of different models in European countries’ Here the majority points to and refers to the announced child-breeding announcement. The majority respond to the notification and assessment of the importance of the stay of children. The members of the Committee of the Progressive Party and the Right do not wish to support a proposal which gives amnesty to illegal immigrants who have not complied with the decision to return to their country of origin. ‘Proposal 3: The Storing asks the Government to review the right of conversion practices to see whether new facts are adequately taken into account’ Here another majority of the committee says that all applications for protection are dealt with individually and thoroughly by UDI and UNE. The majority also refers to the Mæland Committee’s investigation NOU 2010: 12 New appeal mechanism for foreign affairs, where legal certainty issues in the appeal process are under consideration. It shall shortly be sent to the hearing and the majority shall attend to the follow-up to the work of the panel. «Proposal 4: The Government asks the Government to make the necessary changes to the Foreign Affairs Act to make it clear from the text of the law that a refusal to apply must be sound from a child law point of view» This is where the majority of the Committee points out that a new foreign law entered into force on 1 January 2010. The legal position of children has been strengthened through both the proposition, legal provisions and regulatory provisions, including: to ensure that the requirements of the Convention on the Rights of the Child are respected. The majority also refer to the situation of long-term children and the forecasted ageing message in its comments on proposal 2. «Proposal 5: The government asks the government to further specify what falls under the concept of "migration regulatory considerations"» Here the Committee suggests that the government points to Section 38 of the Foreign Affairs Act, where a number of factors have been put into practice, which is the basis for an overall assessment. There the members of the Committee from the Right and the Christian People's Party go in and say that we also want to assess for reasons that move in a positive direction – what is used in Section 38 is essentially restrictive proposals. For example, it should be assessed whether the applicant concerned has acquired training and competence which could move towards a positive treatment of an application for residence. The Right Party and the Christian People's Party have therefore signaled that we want to support this proposal from the Left. «Proposal 6: The government asks the government to ensure that those residing in the country are entitled to health care in addition to emergency care...» Here the Committee points out that the Ministry of Health and Health has sent to the hearing a proposal to amend the priority regulation, which will better deal with this with immediate assistance and health care as is absolutely necessary. «Proposal 7: The government asks the Government to give a new instruction to its Nav offices to deal with emergency aid requests under the Social Services Act in a humane way» Here the Committee refers to the regulation adopted in 2004 and assumes that Nav will follow it. ‘Suggestion 8: The Storting asks the Government to investigate the possibility of a change in its application for a residence permit under Chapter 3 of the Act by aliens who are in the State in the context of an application for protection.’; Here the majority of the committee says that we don't want to put in place a system where you can change queues.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e3600	The policy we pursue in the field of asylum and refugees is very important, because this is about people in need. We have international obligations to accept people who need our protection. We must have a consistent, fair and secure refugee and asylum policy in line with the Soria Moria declaration. So what does that mean? This means, among other things: that we have a competent and effective apparatus capable of dealing with matters within a reasonable time. That means that those who need protection will get it. It means quick return, preferably on a voluntary basis, for those who do not have the right to protection. It is a human right to seek asylum. That is why it is also very important that our asylum policy is clear and predictable. No one has earned that a large number of people without the need for protection still try to stay on this basis. No one has earned the right to remain in Norway without a legal stay. Everyone agrees. Since the red-green government took over in 2005, there has been a systematic effort to reduce the number of groundless applications, to monitor the residual situation of foreign agencies and to speed up the implementation of these rejections. This policy is now beginning to produce results. The number of asylum seekers has decreased, the UDI is dealing for the first time in a long time with more cases than they receive. It will also benefit them to be, in the form of increased resources for faster settlement and better integration. In addition, it will allow for increasing the number of quota refugees, which is perhaps the weakest group of all. The left, with some of its proposals in this case, signals a desire to reverse this policy, with the consequences it will have, among other things. for future asylum seekers. It's a bad signal to give, that just you can stay in Norway long enough without legally staying, you get to stay. This will lead to new growth in applicants without a need for protection. This will result in even more people putting their lives on hold in an attempt to avoid return, instead of using the return programs that exist. This will weaken the legitimacy of asylum policy. It's just those people who need protection who lose it. Legal certainty is weakened if there is a premium to stay illegally in the country. At the same time, it is not difficult to recognise that asylum and refugee policy raises many human dilemmas. Firstly, the long-term child, secondly, the possibility of necessary health care, and thirdly, the right to emergency care. Children are not responsible for the actions of their parents. In the context of Storting's treatment of the new law on foreigners, it was explicitly stated that the best interests of children should be given greater weight than under the old law. This is clearly reflected both in the proposition, in the legislative text, in the debate in Stortinget and in the subsequent regulations. The Convention on the Rights of the Child applies and the Government has initiated its own notification work on children on refugees, assessing further measures. The right to necessary health care is now addressed through planned changes to the priority regulation. The matter has been at the hearing and is being dealt with by the government. In the case of proposal 5, where the Workers' Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Center Party do not wish to participate in all the comments of the Right and Christian People's Party, it is because we believe that the listing made in relation to Section 38 of the Foreign Affairs Act is good enough listing, and we do not see any need for further investigation of the basis of that paragraph. In the case of emergency financial assistance under the social services law, it also includes persons residing illegally in Norway. However, the principle of subsidiarity is interpreted in such a way that persons with other means of living are not entitled to financial benefit. Persons who have applied for asylum shall be offered accommodation. They are therefore not entitled to social benefits from the municipality. Persons who have been refused asylum may be offered accommodation. The Ministry of Justice has instructed the UDI to receive such an offer at the latest in January of this year. They will therefore not be entitled to social benefits from the municipality either. Persons without legal residence who have never applied for asylum in Norway are also not offered accommodation. They will be entitled to emergency assistance and, where appropriate, housing services under the Social Services Act. Finally, a few words about two of the other proposals that are being made: the study of the Mæland Committee is being consulted. The committee has reviewed the appeal procedure in foreign matters. The proposal to switch queues, from asylum seekers to migrant workers, for persons living in Norway, is not accepted. The majority do not want to contribute to the institution of asylum being undermined in this way. The solution adopted in connection with the Maria Amelie case is, in the majority's view, a better solution. Asylum policy is firm. Instead, changes were made to the rules on labour immigration. This case and other cases show that it is both possible and desirable to adjust the legislation if we see that it produces unintended effects. The majority will continue to be a guarantor of a consistent, fair and legally secure refugee and asylum policy.

These are refugees who, until now, have at least had the opportunity to support themselves. They have had tax cards that enable them to be in work. They have also lived in municipalities where the municipality has given them start-up loans, so that they could establish their own housing. All this was taken from them overnight. I was myself and met them in the Cathedral when there was action there. One thing was that they had been deprived of the opportunity to work, but it was the feeling of being deprived of all the rights that really hurt. At some point you have to cut through and say that enough is enough. At some point one must be able to discuss some form of regularisation of this group. It is not about giving residence to absolutely everyone who is staying illegally in the country. The people we're talking about here are the people who've been here for five years or more, who can't be returned. That group might be between 1,500 and 3,000 people. So we're not talking about thousands of thousands. Similar regularisations have been made in Europe without the collapse of the asylum institution in those countries. Why does the Christian People's Party believe that it is wise to consider a regularization of this group? I would like to say at the outset that the Christian People's Party agrees with the majority in this Chamber that asylum seekers who are finally refused and do not meet the criteria for a residence permit in Norway must go home and that it should be done on a voluntary basis. However, the situation is such that there are still some asylum seekers who are not travelling for various reasons. So the authorities that are responsible for for forcing them to return, they don't have the power to do that. As a result, in Norway we get a group of undetectable — or paperless — which is the concept that has been used — that lives on the outside of society. This is an unfortunate situation, both for those involved and for society. It can be hoped that this problem will disappear by itself, for example, that all the paperless will eventually choose to cooperate on returns after they realise that the Norwegian authorities do not want to comply. But in reality, we see that this does not happen. We believe that political authorities have a responsibility to find a solution and to help reduce the number of people who live in Norway without rights, both in the interests of society and the person concerned. I understand the fundamental arguments that going underground is not going to pay off, and that you don't want to put in place schemes that can have major immigration-regulating consequences, but I think this won't be a situation where the whole asylum institution breaks down. ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e3717	Before commenting on the eight proposals that have been put forward – if I reach everything – I would like to underline the basics of the government's asylum policy, which are filled with dilemmas every day and – I kept saying – in every single case. Asylum seekers who fulfil the conditions for protection or residence on a humanitarian basis shall be allowed residence. Those who don't, obligations to go home. Most countries accept their own citizens who want to return, and annual voluntary and assisted returns to a number of countries for which we are currently unable to make forced returns. It's important to underline. We also have support schemes to motivate voluntary return, and we see that the number is growing. Rewarding long-term illegal residence with a residence permit will weaken the entire government's work on asylum policy, including return work. More seriously, granting a residence permit in such a situation would undermine our asylum system and our ability to help people who actually have a real need for protection. I therefore share the Committee's objective that the least possible stay is illegal in Norway and that we must strive for voluntary return arrangements and the use of forced return where possible. I am also pleased that a large majority agree that the stay assessment should distinguish between those who cooperate with the authorities on return and identification and those who do not. So to the proposals: I record that the majority of the committee, like the government, does not see the need for a human rights analysis of the situation of persons without legal residence in Norway, cf. proposal No 1st@item: inlistbox Our international obligations are already incorporated into Norwegian law by the Human Rights Act. In the event of a conflict, the provisions of the Convention prevail before the Norwegian law, and in the event of doubt, the interpretation result which is consistent with the Convention obligation shall be chosen. Furthermore, human rights are assessed by the introduction of legislation in all disciplines. I would also like to remind you that UDI and UNE consider the relevant Convention obligations in their handling of individual cases. Such an analysis could also be complicated in the sense that human rights are individual rights and could be different because of what kind of individual we are facing, and there are many. So this I believe needs to be handled just by UDI and UNE in the individual cases. Furthermore, I am pleased that a broad majority does not support proposal No. 2, that the government should consider granting permanent residence permits to long-term residents in Norway on the basis of an assessment of different models in European countries. As long as the situation is such that a non-resident who has received a final refusal to apply for protection may return voluntarily, the government does not want to reward illegal residence with a residence permit. I would like to stress that Norway is not in the same situation as other European countries that have carried out regularisations in very special situations. I don't have time to go into it now, but there are very special reasons and which I think are clearly not comparable to the situation in Norway. It is also wrong to present it as if Norway should now be one of the strictest regimes in this field. I believe that both the number of asylum seekers, the number of concessions and how we have organised the administration of foreigners – a broad political community – are among the best in Europe. In addition, I would like to remind you that we already have rules that allow a foreigner’s residence time to be weighted in assessing whether there are grounds for staying in Norway. The law allows residence if there are strong human interests or if the foreigner has a special connection. Special attention shall be paid to the link between children and the State. A stay may also be provided if there are practical obstacles to the return of the non-resident. However, the government wants to look at the situation of children who have lived illegally for a long time in Norway. We have notified it, and more people have been involved in it, and that is something that I also sail is very concerned about. Children are not guilty of choosing their parents. The government will provide a children's pooling message before summer. Central issues will be discussed here, including the situation of long-term children and whether there is a basis for embellishment of practice, so that illegal residence times are less than today’s. There are many dilemmas here. We want to go into these. Like the committee, I assume that all applications for protection are dealt with individually and thoroughly by UDI and UNE. I therefore do not see the need for proposal No. 3, on a specific review of practices in transformation cases to see whether new facts are adequately taken into account. ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e3817	I want with this interpellation to raise a debate about a matter I know very many are concerned about, and that has been much discussed in the media. These are mentally ill people who commit repeated crimes but are not liable to criminal law. These people cannot be punished for the crimes they commit because they have a mental illness. At the same time, they're too healthy to be taken care of by the psychiatry. The specialist health service says they have been processed and the municipalities have to take responsibility for them. On the other hand, the municipalities do not have a law to work on the basis of which they can defend coercive measures. We're dealing with a black player game, where the losing party is mentally ill people who become caste balls in a public bureaucracy with multiple levels of government, who just play the ball between themselves and don't take responsibility. In the evening of March 22, we could read about a man who had committed 588 crimes. It said: ‘41-year-olds have been arrested a number of times, but are always released He is too sick to be punished – too healthy to be subjected to forced treatment’ Why is that so? There's probably more answers to that question, but it's close to asking if there was something missing in the big reorganization of the psychiatry. St. Report. No 25 for 1996–1997 and the Plan of Ascendment with earmarked funds for the period 1998–2008 were a necessary reform and contributed to greater transparency about mental illness. Many mentally ill people, who had lived a large part of their lives in an institution, got a new and better life in the many organized homes that were established in the country. In addition to the provision of housing, day-care centres, psychosocial teams were established and followed up with advice and guidance at home and workplaces. St. Report. No 25 for 1996–1997, which was called Transparency and Completeness, and which referred to mental disorders and various services, suggested that everyone should have their own housing in society, as other people do. Psychiatric treatment should range from institution-based treatment to a rotation of more outpatient treatment from specialist healthcare in close cooperation with the community health service. In the context of the psychiatry reform, municipalities have been given more responsibility and new tasks. They have employed personnel with competence in mental health work and have expanded the services of the new target group they have been given. There has been a huge shift from the specialist health service to the municipalities of both tasks and responsibilities, but the municipalities have taken it. According to a report from SINTEF, there has been a reduction of 2 177 daily places in psychiatry during 1991-2006. The slogan has been: the time of asylum is over! Several psychiatric institutions have been reduced, and some are almost debilitated. The intention is no longer to live in an institution. Hospital treatment shall be short-term and temporary, with a clear objective of printing to the home as soon as it is assessed as sound. Most people agree that this has been a proper development, and many, as I said, have got a new and better life out in the municipalities. But even with good plans and many good services in place, there are still some people who don't fix their lives in the 'open' society. They experience living in a kind of ‘grey zone’ and are unable to do so, while the specialist health service says they have been fully treated. These people don't get better, they're chronically ill. Many people are psychotic almost all the time. These are the people you might have forgotten in your heart to reorganize the psychiatry. The ones we're talking about are those seriously ill with low functional levels who have a mental illness in combination with drug abuse those with mental illness and high levels of risk of violence those with mental illness in combination with ADHD, challenging behaviour and poor impulse control; These people need services, follow-up and help, but often do not realize it themselves. Self - insight is often very poor. In serious offences, they are sentenced to psychiatric treatment or sometimes to custody. In the smaller offences, there will be no response. Sometimes they are correctly driven by the police to the emergency ward in psychiatric hospitals when they are obviously psychotic. But they're just there for a few days, and then it's out again. They can't be punished for the crimes they do, because they're not predictable. Formerly, persons with such diagnosis lived and lived much of their lives in the major psychiatric institutions. Asylum was like small communities, with farms, shops, laundry services, horticulture and other crafts. They had both work and housing within the institutional area. The area was open, they could move freely around the municipality, but they got closely followed up when the behavior changed and gave cause for concern. I do not mean by this to say that asylums should be re-emerged. However, there are some elements of structure, follow-up and offer to those seriously mentally ill people who were carriers of asylum culture, which one might want to implement in a new form in the new measures and in all the organised housing being established. Some people need a specialised care most of their lives. They're done with treatment, but they still need close follow-up, because they're sick and have to live on with their severe mental disorder. The unpredictable mentally ill persons who commit repeated criminal acts are not so many. They're a few people, but they make a lot of difference. The police use a lot of resources on them. The same thing happens to the health care system. Family, neighbourhood and networks are often exposed to their unforeseeable behaviour and may be plagued by their criminal acts, which are often stealing, warfare and burglary. According to the Evening Post, the police say that they estimate that there are 25 to 30 criminals in Oslo who have more or less a ‘free ticket’ to commit many less serious but nevertheless atrocious illegal acts without risking punishment. Four-five of them are so active that they have received the name VIC, Very Important Criminals. The Vic Group of the Police is following criminal repeaters very closely. Some of them go in and out of psychosis. Therefore, some in the group may be penalised in some cases. Sometimes they go free. The current rules on criminal inviolability and specific criminal law reactions came into force on 1 January 2002. The former insurance institution was suspended and mental health protection was required to take responsibility for the implementation of special reactions for unaccountable. It was decided that a post-control check would be carried out after a few years. In 2006, the Ministry of Justice, in consultation with the Ministry of Health and Welfare, appointed a working group, the Melandic Group, to monitor the rules on criminal inviolability and specific criminal law reactions. The report was submitted on 30 April 2008. The report states that new instruments are needed to protect society from repeated and plagiaristic criminal acts carried out by improbable persons. The committee also believes that the threshold for condemning a specific criminal reaction should be lowered somewhat. The report was in consultation, with a deadline of 1 April 2009. The Ministry of Justice sent out a new letter of consultation last autumn, in October, with the consultation deadline now this year, on 1 February. This consultation note has been prepared in cooperation with the Ministry of Health and Welfare. This consultation concerns the extension of the scope of specific criminal law responses for unpredictable perpetrators of persistent and socially harmful crime. This was one of the proposals that the Hearing Officers were asked to give their views on in the context of the Mæland report. The hearing responses so far do not give any new signals. It's the same argument that goes on again, with disclaimer from all the bodies involved. Police and criminal services say that one should lower the threshold in order to condemn a particular criminal law reaction with the transfer to forced mental health protection. Psychiatry and health services believe that the measures that society considers necessary to ensure itself against crime need to be anchored in criminal care, and they are opposed to resolving criminal challenges in the field of mental health protection. The municipalities, as a third-party body, are afraid to get the full responsibility in jail and express great scepticism to enter into an agreement on fixed-term forced residence in a municipal institution. We're equidistant. The various bodies continue to shift responsibility from one to the next. It can't go on like that. My question to the Council of State is therefore: Will the Council of State take the initiative to make the necessary legislative changes that make it clear which body is responsible for reaction and follow-up when mentally ill ill people commit repeated crimes?
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e3899	The answer is short and correct: Yes, I will take the initiative – I have taken the initiative. I'm a little surprised at the interpellation in that way. Initiatives are being asked to bring new legislation into place. It was therefore presented by the Ministry of Justice on 22 October last year, which also referred to the interpellant – when a new specific criminal law reaction was proposed and I, at best, tried to cut through a very comprehensive debate and presented ‘temporary forced stay in mental health protection’. The proposal we then sent out, which was based on the original position of the Mæland Committee, was subject to a deadline of 1 February 2011. We are now working, in cooperation with the Ministry of Health and Welfare, to follow up the proposal, in the light of the input of the Hearing Officers. But it's also a pleasure, as the interpellant has noticed, that this reaction has been taken into account in the current year's budget, so that money has actually been allocated to it. It'll be all right, I see. The purpose of the new special reaction and its justification by the government is to facilitate long-term rehabilitation through stabilisation, possible detoxification, the identification of health and social needs and the preparation of a follow-up plan. This is a very complicated group, which I understand that the interpellant also has a great understanding of. It wouldn't help many of these to provide ordinary criminal care. There will be no possibility of coercive treatment, including medics, unless the conditions for the use of compulsory mental health protection are met. Most psychotic people with a high level of crime need comprehensive treatment and may need more services. A good investigation and follow-up requires that it be organised for cooperation and good communication between different relevant social services — which I think the interpellant highlights very well in its interpellation and in its justification — such as the use of drugs. community, specialist, Nav, social, child protection, school, police and criminal services. Proposals for joint preparation of a follow-up plan for convictions and access to communication in this context could contribute to this. The consultation proposal therefore includes a specific exception to the rule that the information concerned must consent to the exchange of confidential information between bodies. It's probably one of those fields where we really see that the obligation of confidentiality can sometimes be used as a reason and as a real seal for effective crime prevention. The maximum length of long-term forced stay in mental health protection is proposed on our side for six months. Six months may be necessary to carry out investigations and possible detoxification. If the investigation and any detoxification have been completed before six months, the special reaction shall cease. The background to the proposal is the existence of a small group of unpredictable individuals, including the interpellant, in an estimated 10 to 20 persons who repeatedly commit very painful criminal offences. Infringements in family relationships, threats and violence against public officials, large-scale acts of warfare, intimidation and perpetrators are examples of such crimes. These cannot be punished. Nor is it the law in force to use the specific criminal law reaction to the transfer to mandatory mental health protection — it does not come to an end. As the representative is aware, the special reactions on the transfer to forced mental health protection and forced care are reserved for the most serious offences that could violate the lives, health or freedom of others. They are timeless and very intrusive and would not really be particularly relevant to those we are talking about here. The group of perpetrators of repeated and perpetrators of crime often does not meet the conditions for admission to forced mental health protection either. Many abilities do not or do not always want to receive health care. It's complicated, it's difficult. It is as the representative points out, a ‘missing link’ or a hole, in the law. The purpose of this consultation proposal is precisely to try to close this gap. In addition, the need to extend the scope of the specific criminal law responses to cases of perpetrators of persistent and socially harmful crime has been assessed several times. In the spring of 2004, a limited back-testing of the specific criminal law responses was carried out for improbable purposes. The check revealed that there is a small group that is incalculable and particularly annoying. In the proposal for the general part of the new penal code promoted in 2004 — i.e. under the previous government — the Ministry pointed out that the best reasons are that solutions should be sought within the healthcare system for this group of unreliable people. Furthermore, the Ministry stated that ‘special reactions should in principle be reserved for offenders who may be dangerous to the lives, health or freedom of others’. The issue was further assessed in the context of ex post verification of the rules on criminal inviolability and specific criminal law reactions. The follow-up was carried out by an investigation team, which has also been involved in the interpellant, the so-called Mæland group, appointed in 2006 by the Ministry of Justice in consultation with the Ministry of Health and Welfare. The report was submitted on 30 April 2008. The Mæland Group notes in its report that new instruments are needed to protect society from repeated and plagiaristic criminal acts carried out by improbable persons. Furthermore, the report states that the threshold for condemning a specific criminal reaction should be lowered somewhat. The Mæland Group does not propose a fully-explored model with concrete legislative proposals, but provides an outline of a possible model of how a possible specific reaction to this group can be implemented. That was why we had to have a new round on this matter and send it out to the hearing in October and make the proposal. The initiative has been taken. At the same time, the Group recommends further consideration of the issue. The report was sent to the hearing on 1 April 2009. There we asked for views on this, and I share, to the extent that the inter-pellant's views on this point of view are shared by the hearing bodies. It often becomes a throwball, I get the impression. However, many hearing bodies supported the proposal to extend the scope of the specific criminal law responses. However, there were shared views on the content of any special reaction, which was closely related to who was actually going to do the job. Someone has to cut through. That is what the Ministry of Justice, in my opinion, has done so far when we have made a proposal, and when we have also chosen through the Health Service to allocate money for this already this year. Some supported the extension of existing legislation, while others wanted a time-limited response. Many of the hearing bodies were also negative or strongly opposed to extending the specific criminal law responses. We face very fundamental counter-conceptions quickly, which are, to a large extent, good counter-conceptions, because they are instruments and reactions that do not work fast and that do not make us bring down crime – either in the short term or in the long term. With this as a hindrance, that proposal for a temporal response was sent to the hearing in October 2010. At the same time, the government aims to extend the current special reaction to the transfer to forced mental health protection and to force care for crimes that are characterised by privacy violations and which repeatedly affect the same person in such a way that it is a form of systematic persecution. This means that persons who are repeatedly subject to criminal offences by a person who is not liable to criminal law are given special protection. Current specific reactions are time-indeterminate and may persist as long as the recurrence risk is present. Finally, I just want to say that I think it is commendable that the interpellant raises this issue in Stortinget. The initiative is taken from our side, but I'm going to be so open and honest and say that I think these are very difficult things, because we have to find responses that work. Besides, it's hard because it's a very vulnerable group, which can easily be hidden away in places where you don't get legal help, but where you get the opposite, and which makes you sit back, and we've really just gotten a derailment from what it's all about -- a rest cushion for society. Then we are quickly involved in instruments that also violate these people's fundamental human rights. I think we've managed to find a balanced solution that activates, that helps society take responsibility and do what you can. And then we'll probably have to experience in many of these things -- even in the future -- that we're not always able to control the human behavior, as we might wish. I thank you for the interpellation.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-05.ana.ud1918e3961	The representative Bente Stein Mathisen places an important theme on the agenda with this interpellation. The essence of the interpellation – or in the question, whether you want to – is how I interpret it: how is society going to deal with mentally ill and unpredictable people who commit criminal acts? It's a good question, but it's also a difficult question, which unfortunately has no simple answer. When a person by definition is likely to commit a criminal offence, he is punished with a sentence, such as a social sentence or a prison sentence. However, people who are psychotic or highly devolved will often be defined as criminally unpredictable at the moment of action and thus cannot be sentenced to ordinary punishment. These are the people we are talking about in this debate. Many of these commit repeated crimes without having direct consequences. The interpellant refers to a report in the evening edition of the Evening Post a few weeks ago, where we read about a person who had committed nearly 600 crimes, without him being punishable. There was a police inspector named Bente Gerner in the Oslo police district who said to the evening edition: ‘The man is psychotic and therefore cannot be punished for the crimes he commits. At the same time, he is not so ill or dangerous to his environment that he can be treated with coercion. He falls between two chairs.’ I don't think we have a good enough system that captures these people, both because society isn't well enough protected from new criminal acts, and because several of those we're talking about here oppose or do not understand the necessity of neither investigation, treatment or follow-up, so that they can change the pattern of action, and thus prevent them from committing new criminal acts. That's what's at the heart of the challenge. That is why I am pleased with what the Minister of Justice said in his comments. The fact that the government will have a law in place, where these mentally ill criminal repeaters will be able to be transferred to mental health protection for a limited stay will, in my view, be an important and appropriate measure. Through such a specific criminal law response as outlined by the Minister of Justice, society will be more likely to interfere with the individual’s patterns of action. One will be able to determine, motivate and initiate current treatment in relation to the mental health status of the criminal. He or she can get help with his or her drug problem through detoxification and rehabilitation. If one really succeeds, one can gain understanding of the criminal in order for society to react to the criminal act, thus gaining understanding that criminal acts are unacceptable and that society must set limits on what is allowed and not allowed. Cooperation between mental health protection and relevant services such as the community's health and care services, the Nav, police and criminal services requires the identification of health and social needs, thus facilitating both a scheme to break the criminal pattern of action and the continuation of a scheme for individuals following the implementation of the specific criminal law reaction. Here, too, the keywords are fixed housing, close follow-up through employment and further treatment, rehabilitation and rehabilitation. In the long term, this will hopefully help to reduce criminal behaviour. The goal is that an overall arrangement around the individual will prevent those we are talking about from falling between the two chairs that police inspector Bente Gerner refers to. In a pictorial way, society, instead of individual chairs, must have a whole and coherent platform of measures to ensure that the mentally ill and unreliable criminals receive treatment and follow-up, while at the same time protecting society from the criminal acts they commit. Such a long bench of action is resource-intensive, but absolutely necessary to protect individuals and society. I see that I have some time left, and I'd like to ask a question to the interpellant and the Right: what do they mean they have to do beyond what the government is doing now, in order for the unpredictable mental illness not to fall between the two chairs, or to be ‘throat balls’ in the system, as the interpellant himself described in his post?

Finally, when it comes to identifying who will be responsible for the reaction and follow-up of mentally ill offenders, whether they become the criminal services, the psychiatry or the municipalities, the necessary resources must also be made available for the economy and professionals. It is then also gratifying to hear that the Council of State was involved in this in its statement, that it is actually set of funds in the current year's budget, and that there will probably be more of it. Because there is no doubt that it is a cost-intensive task to be held responsible for the unencumbered mentally ill people under criminal law. I suspect that the economic challenges have also played a major role in addition to the legal ones when the authorities concerned have tried to shift responsibility for these people to others. With regard to the question raised by the representative, Tove-Lise Torve, the Right considers that a decision must be made first and foremost on who should be responsible for those falling between two chairs. Personally, I guess it's close to pointing out that it must be the specialist health service of the psychiatry that should have a special responsibility for this group, in close cooperation, which the council also had in mind, with the municipalities and Nav. There is a special care provision and follow-up to these people needs, and the specialist healthcare service must take on specific responsibilities. The right has also proposed a new and reinforced quality strategy for healthcare, and we also call for ways to ensure better quality in mental health protection.

Often it doesn't matter. This has been a worthy and constructive debate on an incredibly important issue for my part. I understand that there are several parties involved in it, and I am pleased about that. As the representative Graham was about, it is about the treatment of people, and the debate has brought it up. I would therefore like, when the proposal is clear from the government, to invite all the parties of the Storting to join in and discuss just the new form of reaction, because I think it needs to be brought together – broader collection than just a majority. For it needs more legitimacy than maybe other kinds of solutions, and accepted so that people understand how difficult it is and also understand why it should be reacted like that. Then we need a broad majority. Only that will be understood, and only that will be effective. That we are serious, is also underlined by the budget, where, for the current year, we have chosen to allocate a total of 12 million. kr to establish a new special reaction. But there's also 3 million. SEK for a limited period of compulsory residence in the specialist health service and 6 million has been allocated. kr for municipal follow-up, and SEK 3 million. kr was granted for pilot cooperation between specialist health services, municipal authorities, police, criminal services and Nav pending the establishment of the scheme.
